Bally's · BALY-US · the new Memo conveyor's card of 30 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Bally's

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 51,376 words, set out under Richard's eleven ratings in the order of the eleven ratings page: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the eleven ratings page uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external environment is negative and getting worse at the margin in the three places that matter most for Bally's: the cost of debt, Chicago's municipal politics and UK fiscal policy. Demand itself is not the problem. US regional demand is mature and softening gently, and UK online demand is still growing. The problem is that every external force now moving is moving against a balance sheet with no cushion. Rates and credit spreads set the price of the refinancing due by the 31 March 2027 waiver cliff. Chicago legalised video gaming terminals before the permanent casino opened. The UK doubled the duty on the subsidiary's highest-margin business. PENN doubled its Chicagoland capacity. The only external tailwinds (UK consolidation, the Rhode Island monopoly, and capital from landlords and private credit) either accrue to a ring-fenced, 58%-owned subsidiary or come at a price that transfers value to capital providers.

For the Consumer: Model 2027 US regional organic revenue at 0% to +2%. Assume no external relief. Treat the next six months as the window in which external forces are most likely to produce negative surprises: the credit market at the refinancing, the Chicago video-terminal and licence questions around the 10 December 2026 deadline, and the UK generosity cuts in H2 2026.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0230C-0518C-0712C-0925C-3528C-3928
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

F3027th of 28

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Competitive intensity is rising in both of Bally's US arenas: the Chicago land-based market and US online. In the UK it is changing shape in favour of scale, which helps Bally's Intralot. Net, it is rising against Bally's, and Bally's lacks the capital to respond.

  • Chicagoland is the critical front. PENN reinvested over $500m in three years. Its Joliet relocation (August 2025) raised slot volumes 1.4 times and table volumes 2.0 times. Its Aurora relocation (24 June 2026) nearly doubled admissions and slot volumes. That capacity arrives while Bally's temporary casino is underperforming and before its permanent casino opens. Chicago's legalisation of video gaming terminals, on which Accel already holds 44% of location approvals, removes the in-city slot exclusivity Bally's believes its host agreement gave it.
  • Elsewhere in the US: Bally's lost 30 basis points of Missouri share in August 2026; Atlantic City revenue keeps contracting under pressure from larger operators; new openings and promotions hit Shreveport and Dover; Resorts World Queens became a full commercial casino; and competitor refreshes in under-invested markets such as Kansas City would force defensive reinvestment that Bally's cannot fund.
  • US online is a spending war Bally's has left. In Q2 2026 DraftKings' sales and marketing spend was $311m (+40%) and FanDuel's $353m (from $219m). Fanatics took its share from 6.1% to 9.5% between December 2025 and April 2026. Prediction markets bidding for search keywords have raised sportsbook customer acquisition costs by about 40%. North America Interactive turned profitable by cutting marketing, which is consistent with flat share rather than gains.
  • In the UK the 40% duty is a tax on lack of scale. Operators below about £100m to £133m of revenue are expected to exit, and Tier 1 operators have taken 7 percentage points of share since 2021. Bally's International Interactive (Bally's Intralot B2C) holds 14% to 15% of UK online casino and spends 11% of revenue on marketing against Evoke's 23%, so it gains, and the Evoke deal would make it number two in UK iGaming. Against that, Flutter is raising promotional generosity in H2 2026, and the CEO concedes consolidation has "not moved as quickly" as expected.

For the model: Assume no share gains in US land-based markets, share loss risk in Chicago and Missouri, flat online share in North America, and share gains in UK online offset by a promotional arms race.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

F1828th of 28

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: A rising headwind. By far the most important input is capital, whose price has risen to 11% to 13% for Bally's. That arrives below EBITDA and in rent, where it cannot be passed on.

  • Labour: about 34% of the workforce is unionised under 32 collective agreements, and wages keep creeping up. In 2024 regional operators guided property margins down about 220 basis points on labour (Deutsche Bank).
  • Construction: Chicago's minimum commitment is $1.34bn, with about $400m still to spend at June 2026. Management says actual costs will exceed the minimum, and experts put total cost above $1.7bn. With construction contracts still unexecuted, overruns cannot yet be estimated. Tariff exposure is limited, since steel is sourced domestically and only the curtain wall comes from India.
  • Suppliers are concentrated: the three largest slot makers hold 75% to 76% of the North American floor, and Light & Wonder raises lease prices 3% to 5% a year. Minimum commitments run to $41.1m for interactive technology through 2030 and $96.8m for sponsorships through 2036.
  • Capital is the dominant input. The parent issued 11% first-lien notes due 2028 and borrowed for the Bronx at SOFR plus 850 basis points. Unsecured bonds trade at secondary yields of up to 12.9%. Each 100 basis points on floating debt costs about $22.7m a year. Euro high-yield bond yields are up 90 basis points since September (FfF). GLPI capital carries an 8.5% yield plus escalators. The $200m sterling swap expired in October 2026.
  • Energy: higher gasoline prices are hitting drive-in visitation across regional markets (Deutsche Bank, October 2026).

For the model: Hold Casinos & Resorts margins flat to down 50 basis points absent Chicago; raise interest cost on any refinancing; and add a cost-overrun range to Chicago.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

F1228th of 28

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Macro bites hardest at the company level, through the cost and availability of debt. The sector and industry feel it through the consumer; Bally's feels it through refinancing.

  • Rates and credit (company level): the US ten-year yield is above 5% after a hawkish Fed hold, euro high-yield bond yields are up 90 basis points since September, and Goldman Sachs' Risk Appetite Indicator is at its lowest since April 2025 (FfF, 30 October 2026). Bally's must refinance or resolve its covenants by 31 March 2027 in this market. Consolidated net interest of about $476m a year is about 15% of revenue (calculated), against an average of 1.85% of sales in the FfF's sample.
  • Consumer (sector level): energy-led pressure on low- and middle-income drive-in customers; Morgan Stanley forecasts flat US gross gaming revenue in 2027.
  • Industry level: Consumer discretionary is Double Underweight and the gambling sector Underweight in our framework; Flutter is down 62% and Entain 39% over twelve months.
  • Currency (company level): sterling is about 28% of revenue. A 10% move in sterling shifts consolidated EBITDA by about 4% and EPS by about 6% (calculated in the source). Morgan Stanley forecasts sterling at $1.27 by Q2 2027; ING sees it flat at $1.35. Most of the sterling earnings sit in the ring-fenced subsidiary, while the parent's debt service is in dollars.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Four other dynamics matter, and on balance they are negative for common equity even where they support the operating company.

  1. GLPI, the landlord, both supports and constrains. It has funded $640.7m of its $940m Chicago commitment (to 30 September 2026) and advanced $48.5m on the Tropicana site, and it has every incentive to keep a 2.08x-covered tenant operating. But on 29 October 2026 it warned publicly that the covenant waiver raises going-concern risk and that a breach could trigger acceleration. Jefferies reports GLPI's real-estate funding postponed to late 2027. Its master leases forbid deferring rent. GLPI protects its rent, not Bally's equity.
  2. Private credit is the lender of last resort. WhiteHawk funded the Bronx ($400m term loan plus a $160m delayed draw at SOFR plus 850 basis points) and also refinanced Star. Capital is available, at a price that moves value to lenders.
  3. Standard General's capacity and intent are an external variable in their own right (C-87).
  4. Legal and investigative overhangs: the Pomerantz investor investigation (September 2026); an SEC investigation reported by Zacks, a weak source the Consumer should verify; and the anti-affirmative-action lawsuit threatening the Chicago IPO financing (Axios).

Two lesser points: Star Entertainment's distress affects the value of the stake; and trading is very thin (about $1.3m a day, short interest near zero since early 2025), so flows can move the price far more than fundamentals.

C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

F2828th of 28

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The biggest new-entrant threat is an adjacent entrant at Bally's most valuable asset: video gaming terminal operators in Chicago.

  • Adjacent: Accel has 44% of Chicago location approvals. The Illinois Gaming Board expects $65m to $100m a year from Chicago terminals. Bally's projects a $260m annual tax loss from its casino; that figure is Bally's own advocacy, not a forecast to model, and the Risks digest's "40-50% revenue cannibalisation" reading is unsupported. Even a fraction of it would materially dent the permanent casino's slot win. Threat: high.
  • Standard entrants: new casinos in New York (Resorts World, Hard Rock) and Illinois expansion (PENN's relocations count as capacity). Threat: medium.
  • Orthogonal entrants: prediction markets (Kalshi, Polymarket), sweepstakes and skill games (Pace-O-Matic in Pennsylvania). Threat: medium for sports betting, low for Bally's mix.
  • UK: offshore and crypto operators. Threat: medium and rising.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

F2827th of 28

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The key suppliers are suppliers of capital and real estate. They are currently a support, conditional and on their own terms, and they are the largest source of risk.

  • GLPI:
  • Support: $640.7m of the $940m Chicago funding was disbursed by 30 September 2026, and $274m of reimbursements in H1 2026 eased capex.
  • Risk: it holds three master leases covering $4.12bn of minimum payments, with escalators; it warned of insolvency risk on 29 October 2026; its funding was reportedly postponed to late 2027; and a lease default would cross-default.
  • Lenders: the term-loan lenders granted the waiver to 31 March 2027, conditional on liquidity maintenance; WhiteHawk provided Bronx money at SOFR plus 850.
  • Operating suppliers:
  • Slot vendors raise lease prices 3% to 5% a year, and switching casino management systems costs millions.
  • White Hat and Kambi are variable-cost but carry minimums ($41.1m through 2030).
  • Evolution dominates live casino.
  • Deferred-payable trade finance is unwinding ($47.0m at December 2025 to $27.7m at June 2026), a cash drain.
  • Payables fell while receivables rose, so suppliers are being paid faster: weaker leverage over suppliers.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0132C-0338C-0415C-0830C-3235C-3715C-4340
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

F3227th of 28

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Demand is not changing structurally in the US casinos and North American online businesses (about 72% of revenue, all in US dollars). It is softening cyclically, and visibility is low. The one structurally growing demand pool, UK online, sits inside the ring-fenced subsidiary and is being taxed. No cyclical swing has two or more years of visibility.

  • US regional casinos are mature and decelerating. Nine-state gross gaming revenue grew 3.5% in Q2 2026 (Jefferies) and 3.6% in Q3, but only 2.5% in September, down from mid-single digits earlier in the year (Deutsche Bank, October 2026). Pennsylvania fell 3.2% and New Jersey 1.3% in September, and Missouri fell 3.4% in August. Illinois (+12.7%) and Louisiana (+11.8%) were lifted by relocations, Bally's Baton Rouge among them. Morgan Stanley forecasts domestic gross gaming revenue flat in 2027 and US real consumer spending growth slowing from 2.3% to 1.8%; University of Michigan sentiment is 46.3, with current conditions the lowest in the survey's history.
  • Bally's own data show the K-shape. Rated visitation rose 4.3% in Q2 2026, while lower-tier and unrated play fell; management deliberately shed that unprofitable play. Casinos & Resorts revenue rose only 2.0%, and landside conversions supplied most of that. Concentration on high-value players (over 60% of rated casino revenue; the top 20% of customers supply about 80%) cushions volume but makes results more exposed to hold and to losing individual players.
  • North American online demand is still growing but slowing. Industry iGaming growth slowed to 13% in September from 16% in Q2. Only seven states have legal online casino. Bally's North America Interactive grew revenue 35.9% in Q1 and 16.9% in Q2.
  • UK online demand is robust but taxed. Constant-currency growth was 10.5% in Q1 and 11.6% in Q2, with net gaming revenue at an all-time high. That was before the H2 2026 cuts to player bonuses and return-to-player. Regulus (reported by Deutsche Bank on 21 August 2026) sees UK gaming revenue possibly falling 12% in 2027. In the Netherlands, a 34.2% tax cut regulated gross gaming revenue by 23% to 25%.

For the model: Casinos & Resorts organic revenue of 0% to +2% in 2027 excluding Chicago, with a downside skew over the next 12 months. Bally's Intralot B2C growth should decelerate from 11-12% as the generosity cuts land, and 2027 is at risk. The cone of outcomes is wide in the UK and narrow but skewed down in US land-based demand.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

F3826th of 28

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Disruption forces are strengthening and act against Bally's, but mostly indirectly and at moderate magnitude. The one that matters most for Bally's money is UK offshore leakage, which the duty strengthens. Chicago's video gaming terminals are best treated as a regulatory disruption of Bally's most important asset (C-04, C-35).

  • Prediction markets are strengthening fast: an annualised $930bn of US notional volume in September 2026 (Rothschild & Co Redburn), 57% of digital sports-betting advertising impressions, and about $300m each committed by DraftKings and Flutter. Bally's sportsbook is small, and its online earnings are led by iGaming, including its Rhode Island monopoly. A federal rule keeps casino games out of prediction markets (sector digest), so the direct revenue hit is small. The indirect hit is that inflated customer acquisition costs slow North America Interactive's scaling.
  • UK offshore and crypto leakage is strengthening. UK offshore gross gambling yield has tripled to £685m since 2019. Cutting bonuses and return-to-player to recover the duty pushes high-value players offshore; the Dutch precedent is a 25% contraction of the regulated market.
  • Sweepstakes are being disproved by state bans (California AB831), a mild positive. Cashless payments and AI-driven CRM help operators that can fund them; Bally's can fund little.

For the model: Hold a 0% to 2% annual drag on North America Interactive growth from acquisition-cost inflation, and model UK leakage as a downside scenario for Bally's Intralot B2C in 2027 rather than the base case.

C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

F1528th of 28

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Regulatory and political change is extensive and overwhelmingly negative. It bites hardest at two levels: municipal Chicago, which hits the single most important development asset, and UK fiscal policy, which hits the highest-margin earnings stream. Bally's has little political capital with which to resist.

  • UK: Remote Gaming Duty rose from 21% to 40% on 1 April 2026. The gross hit was $39m in Q2 2026 and is about €120m a year (Deutsche Bank); 65% was offset in Q2. Remote General Betting Duty rises from 15% to 25% in April 2027; that is smaller for Bally's Intralot, whose business is casino- and bingo-led. Machine Games Duty of 25% will cost Evoke's shops about £9m to £12m a year once Evoke is acquired. This is the third UK gambling duty rise in twelve months, justified on harm grounds, which makes further rises easier to repeat (FfF digest).
  • Chicago: the City Council's legalisation of video gaming terminals (August 2026), which Bally's says breaches its host community agreement; the hotel tower and non-gaming build paused; a 12-month extension of the temporary licence being sought; a host-agreement completion deadline of 10 December 2026 with daily liquidated damages; a city tax on online sports betting raised from 2% to 10.25%, with a proposal to double it to 20%; Illinois' per-wager tax; and opposition to Bally's requested property-tax relief. The Illinois Gaming Board's May 2025 cease-construction order (an unapproved waste-removal vendor) shows how much leverage regulators hold over the project.
  • New York: the $500m licence fee is paid, an independent compliance monitor is required, and a parkland alienation bill for the Ferry Point site is still needed.
  • Rhode Island: the online monopoly through 2043 is the one positive, but the regulatory agreement restricts dividends and other restricted payments while leverage exceeds 5.5x, prohibits expansion into neighbouring states, and leaves $39.0m of capex still to be invested.
  • Other: state sports-betting tax rises (Maryland 20%, New Jersey 19.75%, Louisiana 21.5%); a A$260m anti-money-laundering penalty on Star Entertainment, in which Bally's holds about 38%.

For the model: Hard-code the UK duty steps; carry Chicago as a scenario variable, covering the outcome of the video-terminal dispute, the temporary licence and liquidated damages; and assume no regulatory relief anywhere.

C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

F3028th of 28

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: None is accelerating Bally's demand. US online legalisation has stalled, US online growth is slowing and regional gaming revenue is decelerating. The only accelerating driver is UK tax-forced consolidation, which speeds Bally's Intralot's share gains by shrinking rivals rather than growing the market, and which accrues to the 58%-owned subsidiary. Rhode Island online casino (+23.3% year on year in July 2026) is a state-specific monopoly, not an industry driver. Chicago's permanent casino is company-specific new supply, not an industry tailwind.

C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

F3526th of 28

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Customer demand supports a stable base, but not the growth and deleveraging thesis, and the support is fragile because it rests on a small group of high-value customers and on the Chicago ramp-up.

  • Supportive: rated visitation up 4.3%; tracked visitors spending more; Rhode Island online casino up 23.3%; UK online up 11.6% in constant currency with no extra marketing; North America Interactive app downloads up 46% year on year in June 2026 (World Cup).
  • Fragile:
  • Over 60% of rated revenue comes from VIPs, and high-value retention has proved sensitive to staff turnover (Atlantic City, 2024).
  • Lower-tier spending is weak.
  • The temporary Chicago casino is below targets, and parking and access limit it.
  • The Washington Bridge closures cost about $2m a quarter in Rhode Island.
  • A former competitor says Bally's "lost customer loyalty" through slow technology upgrades on its casino floors.
  • UK customers face cuts to bonuses and return-to-player in H2 2026, and the Dutch precedent shows volume losses.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

F1528th of 28

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: They are hurting, and the position is very fragile (C-04 gives the detail).

  • Hurting: UK HM Treasury (Remote Gaming Duty to 40%, Betting Duty to 25%, Machine Games Duty to 25%); Chicago City Council (video terminals, online sports-betting tax, opposed property-tax relief); Illinois (per-wager tax); New York (licence fee, compliance monitor, parkland bill); the Illinois Gaming Board (the cease-construction order, now licensing terminals); Australia's AUSTRAC (Star).
  • Helping: Rhode Island regulators protect the monopoly through 2043, though with dividend and expansion restrictions.
  • Why the position is fragile:
  • Bally's needs regulatory goodwill on four live issues at once: Chicago's temporary-licence extension and the dispute over the host agreement; New York's parkland bill; Rhode Island's leverage-linked restrictions; and UK duty policy.
  • A going-concern company has little leverage with regulators.
  • Gaming regulators also judge financial suitability.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F4026th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: The addressable market is not the constraint; capital is.

  • The markets: US regional gaming grows roughly in line with nominal GDP or slower, with Morgan Stanley flat for 2027. US online is forecast to exceed $40bn by 2028 (Morgan Stanley), but is held back by legislation and dominated by two operators. Global iGaming is projected to grow at 12% a year to 2029 (H2 Gambling Capital), and the UK online market at about 1.8% a year to 2030. The Chicago and New York metropolitan markets are large in absolute terms.
  • What that allows for Bally's mid-term organic growth:
  • Casinos & Resorts: 0% to 3% a year, plus a one-off Chicago step-up of perhaps $100m to $200m of revenue above the temporary casino once stabilised. That is my inference: stabilised EBITDAR of $200m to $250m implies revenue several hundred million dollars higher than the temporary casino.
  • North America Interactive: high single digits to low teens.
  • UK online: mid-single digits after the duty.
  • Lottery business-to-business: flat to down until Ohio and Illinois are resolved.
1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0628C-1020
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

F2827th of 28

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Reported revenue is in an acquisition-driven upswing (+28.3% in Q1 2026, +20.5% in Q2), while organic revenue in the core is flat and profit after fixed charges is turning down. The real turn, in profit after rent, depends on the permanent Chicago casino and is not due before 2027-28.

  • What drove the revenue: Bally's Intralot B2B ($79.5m against $7.0m a year earlier), B2C (+22%), the Queen properties and Sam's Town Shreveport (from August 2026, when its gross gaming revenue fell 29.1% during the ownership transition). Organically, Casinos & Resorts rose 2.0%, the UK rose 11.6% in constant currency and North America Interactive 16.9%.
  • Profit is moving the other way. In Q2 2026 adjusted EBITDA fell 4.0% to $124.0m and its margin from 19.7% to 15.7% (calculated). Rent rose 45% to $63.5m, after the Lincoln sale-leaseback added $56m a year. Net interest rose 22% to $119.0m. Operating cash flow was -$265.9m in H1 2026.
  • The next six months are flat to down: the first full half of the UK duty, Sam's Town's transition, the temporary Chicago casino and the waiver cliff.

For the model: Treat 2026 as the trough year for profit after rent only if Chicago opens on time and the UK mitigation completes; otherwise the trough extends into 2027.

C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

F2028th of 28

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: The headwinds are strong and broad; the tailwinds are weak and confined to the ring-fenced UK business. The overall external picture is clearly negative for Bally's parent equity.

  • Regulated online growth (cohort driver): a weakening tailwind. US legislation has stalled; UK growth is intact but now taxed. A small tailwind at the value-chain level, through share gains for Bally's Intralot; neutral to negative at sector level.
  • Gaming taxes: a strong headwind at both sector and company level (UK Remote Gaming Duty and Betting Duty, Chicago's online sports-betting tax, state sports-betting rates).
  • Customer acquisition: a headwind in the US, where prediction markets and the leaders' spending inflate costs; some relief in the UK as weak operators exit.
  • Sports results: random and neutral, with little exposure.
  • Land-based regional demand: a cyclical headwind on a structurally stable base.
  • Value chain: landlords and suppliers capture profit, and lenders price Bally's risk at 11% to 13%.

The FfF tilts agree: Industry Double Underweight, Sector Underweight, Fragility Double Underweight. Bally's is the archetype of what the regime sells: geared, small, complex and unproven.

Internal change forces/risks

–not yet rated

Memo topic 2, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Internal change is extensive but mostly defensive or value-transferring. The genuinely positive changes (North America Interactive moving into profit, the landside conversions, Bally's Intralot's UK mitigation and the Chicago casino's construction progress) are execution wins in operations. They sit alongside a governance and capital-allocation pattern that keeps adding complexity, fixed charges and related-party exposure: the Queen merger, Sam's Town, the Bronx licence, Star, and buying the subsidiary's shares during a going-concern warning. The decisive internal change is the concentration of control in Standard General (Executive Chair, about 67%) and the loss of the finance team. Together these make a controlling-shareholder-led capital-structure event the most likely next step.

For the Consumer: No internal change is large enough to close the parent's fixed-charge gap before Chicago stabilises (2028 at the earliest). Model internal improvements as small, back-ended and partly leaking to minorities, and treat the capital-structure outcome (refinance, recapitalise or restructure) as an explicit scenario switch, not a footnote.

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Mixed. Operating standards are improving in specific pockets; financial-control and governance standards are deteriorating. For forecasting, the deterioration matters more, because it lowers the reliability of reported numbers.

  • Improving:
  • North America Interactive moved from a $40.2m segment loss in FY2024 to a $5.0m loss in the FY2025 successor period, then to +$3.0m of EBITDAR in Q2 2026 on 16.9% revenue growth (automation and CRM under Sina Miri).

  • Database marketing lifted rated visitation 4.3%.
  • About $15m of annualised corporate savings came after Intralot.
  • Bally's Intralot offset 65% of the Q2 UK duty with €11.3m of cost savings and €10.1m of growth.
  • Deteriorating:
  • Disclosure controls were judged ineffective at 31 March 2026, and there is a material weakness in tax accounting controls (Q2 2026), on top of earlier weaknesses in FX accounting under ASC 830 and restatements (2021-22 financials; 2022 cash balances).
  • Quarterly filings were late twice in 2026 (notices on 11 May and 11 August).
  • The executive team keeps turning over (C-11).
  • Compensation discretion has been used to override targets: the 2023 $557m EBITDA target was abandoned and 100% of PSUs paid anyway.
  • Construction compliance has slipped (the Illinois cease-construction order of May 2025), and deferred-payable trade finance has been used to stretch payables.

C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

F1228th of 28

Judgement: Incongruent. The goals (metropolitan mega-resorts, global digital champion) are incongruent with the capital structure. The culture is fractured between a collaborative Gamesys heritage and a top-down, New York-driven parent. Execution is cost-cutting while strategy expands.

  • Expert evidence: "a cutthroat, brutal American culture from New York replaced the collaborative Gamesys environment overnight"; "New York dictated a completely wrong sports betting strategy."
  • Restructuring: up to 15% of interactive staff in 2023; repeated restructuring charges.
  • Pay: governed by discretion rather than outcomes (C-63).

Group judgement

Risks

F728th of 28

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The risk profile is dominated by one correlated cluster: liquidity, covenants and refinancing. It is a base case, not a tail. Covenant waivers expire on 31 March 2027. Management does not project meeting liquidity maintenance over the next twelve months. The parent runs a structural fixed-charge deficit. The landlord has publicly warned of acceleration. Construction, Chicago regulation, UK duty and accounting-control risks all feed into that cluster rather than offsetting it (the Risks digest's "multiple failure pathways reinforce rather than offset"). The core growth engine is decelerating, so operations cannot outgrow the problem in time.

The equity is geared about eight times to enterprise value, so outcomes are binary. On a sum-of-the-parts at fair trough multiples the common equity has little or no value; its market value reflects options on Chicago, the Bally's Intralot stake and Standard General's intentions. The upside tail (a take-private or an equity injection by Standard General at a premium) is real, given its history of bids, but cannot be relied on, and its last bid priced at $15 to $18.25 only after an activist fight.

For the Consumer: Make the capital-structure outcome the first branch of every forecast: refinance at higher cost; dilutive recapitalisation by Standard General; asset-sale bridge; or restructuring. Attach the operating model to each branch.

C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

F628th of 28

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: Several high-magnitude risks are no longer low-probability. The most important (a covenant or liquidity event) is the base risk, not a tail risk. The true tails cluster around Chicago and regulatory or legal shocks.

  1. Covenant breach or failed refinancing, leading to acceleration and cross-default across the master leases and debt. Plausibility: high (going concern; waiver to 31 March 2027; GLPI's warning of 29 October 2026). Magnitude: equity could be wiped out or heavily diluted, consistent with Jefferies' worst case of $1.
  2. The Chicago licence or host agreement: the temporary licence lapses before the permanent casino opens, the liquidated-damages dispute (from 10 December 2026) escalates, or the city and Bally's fight over the host agreement. Plausibility: medium. Magnitude: high, since Chicago is the flagship.
  3. Chicago construction halts or overruns (scope, labour, financing litigation, GLPI funding timing). Plausibility: medium. Magnitude: high.
  4. A Rhode Island shock: the monopoly is liberalised, or the regulatory agreement is breached through leverage restrictions. Plausibility: low. Magnitude: high, since it is the most durable asset.
  5. UK fiscal and regulatory: a further duty rise, or offshore leakage like the Netherlands' (regulated gaming revenue -23% to -25%), which would damage Bally's Intralot's value and Bally's 58% stake. Plausibility: low to medium. Magnitude: medium to high.
  6. Accounting or legal: a restatement or SEC action, given the material weaknesses and the Zacks report. Plausibility: low to medium. Magnitude: medium to high, since it would block market access.
  7. Bally's Intralot covenant or downgrade after Evoke (Fitch Rating Watch Negative; 6.0x pro forma). Plausibility: medium. Magnitude: medium.
  8. Star Entertainment insolvency: write-off of the stake. Plausibility: medium. Magnitude: low to medium.
  9. Sterling collapse: a 10% fall costs about 4% of EBITDA. Plausibility: low to medium.
  10. Standard General's own capacity: limited by the size of its fund.
D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

D459th of 15

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: The drivers are highly concentrated, and most of them are dated and contractual rather than operational. Four items explain nearly all of the difference between FY2025 and FY2028 in the base case: rent steps (about +$156m against FY2025, including Lincoln from February 2026 and Chicago from opening), the repricing and growth of debt (about +$190m of interest against FY2025), the UK duty (a gross hit of $110m to $140m a year, about 65% mitigated), and the Chicago ramp (the only one with a wide range). Against that, the differences in the operating lines the market usually argues about, regional demand, pricing, cost programmes, North America Interactive, together move EBITDA after rent by perhaps $20m to $40m either way over the whole period. Concentration of this kind cuts both ways for predictability. It is a positive because three of the four drivers are already visible in contracts, filings and law, so the direction and much of the size of the change are knowable in advance and need no judgement about customers or competitors. It is a negative because the forecast then leans on one execution variable (Chicago) and one counterparty variable (the refinancing terms by 31 March 2027), each of which is closer to binary than to a distribution, and because the two are correlated: a weak Chicago ramp worsens the financing terms and the political loop at the same time. On balance I score it slightly below neutral: the drivers are few and observable, but the two that set the width of the cone are the two the company controls least.

1. The drivers, sized and classified (base case, FY2025 to FY2028, USD m, EBITDA after rent unless stated).

Driver Size of its contribution to the change Dated or contractual? Observable before it happens? Width of its own range over 18 months Share of the FY2027 EBITDA-after-rent cone it explains (my estimate)
Rent steps: Lincoln +$56m (February 2026), Kansas City and Shreveport +$32m (December 2024), Chicago about +$80m at opening, escalators about 1.5% a year about -$156m to FY2028 Yes: master leases, GLPI filings, CPI floor 1% and cap 2% Yes, to the dollar; only Chicago's start date is uncertain Narrow in size; one to two quarters in timing (each quarter of Chicago rent is about $20m) about 15% (timing only)
Interest: covenant reset repricing, the WhiteHawk loan, new money at about 13%, refinancing the 2029 notes about +$190m of interest to FY2028 (reported; about $85m a year of it non-cash accretion to 2029) Partly: the term loan margin (SOFR plus 7.50%), the WhiteHawk margin (SOFR plus 8.50%) and the swaps are fixed; the reset terms and the new money are not Direction yes; size depends on terms set by lenders Wide below EBITDA: each 100 basis points on about $3bn of parent debt is $30m or about $0.49 a share; each $100m of extra debt at 13% is $13m Not in EBITDA; about half of the EPS cone
UK Remote Gaming Duty to 40% (April 2026) and General Betting Duty to 25% (April 2027) about -$75m to -$90m net of mitigation (B2C margin from 37% to 38% before the duty to 29% to 30%) Yes: statute Yes; the mitigation rate (65% in Q2 2026) and the player response are the uncertain parts Moderate: B2C EBITDAR of $290m could run $30m either side depending on leakage and generosity about 20%
Chicago's ramp: year one at 65% of $500m of stabilised revenue and a 20% margin; stabilised EBITDAR $150m +$75m of EBITDAR to FY2028 against the temporary casino No: execution Partly, after opening, through the Illinois Gaming Board's monthly receipts and Bally's Chicago, Inc.'s own 10-Qs Very wide: Deutsche Bank's first-year base of $160m against our annualised $65m; a late opening lowers rent and raises EBITDA in FY2027 while cutting cash about 50%
Everything else: core casino volumes and margin (0% growth, -30bp), Sam's Town, North America Interactive (+$16m), B2B lottery (Ohio -$20m), corporate costs, synergies inside margins net about +$9m No Yes, monthly state data Modest: about $25m either way about 15%

The last row is the whole of what a normal regional-casino forecast argues about, and here it is the smallest driver.

2. Why the concentration is structural, not an artefact of the model. The company converted its property into rent (sale-leasebacks of Lincoln, Kansas City, Shreveport, the Chicago land) and its equity into debt (the 2025 tender funded with 11% notes, then the $1.1bn term loan, then WhiteHawk), so the lines that move are the lines those contracts created. In FY2021 fixed charges (rent plus interest) were about $140m; in FY2026 they are about $725m; in FY2027 about $884m. EBITDAR roughly doubled over the same period. The forecast differences are therefore the arithmetic of those contracts meeting a flat operating base. The AlphaSense driver-dispersion report reaches the same place from the credit side: "high driver concentration, leaving EPS heavily sensitive to Chicago opening delays and rising financing costs", with the rent and interest tranches rated high-visibility and the Chicago timeline and refinancing execution rated low.

3. Concentration at the equity line is higher still. Below EBITDA, two drivers decide EPS and value: the interest line (counterparty-set) and the share count (branch-set; a recapitalisation could add 50% to 100% to the count). The operating drivers barely register: a 5% swing in core casino revenue moves EPS by about $0.60, a 300 basis point difference in refinancing spread moves it by about $1.50, and branch B changes the denominator by about 62m shares. For the memo's purposes, the equity case is a two-variable problem, Chicago and financing, with the UK as the third.

4. Comparison with peers. At Boyd (90% owned property, 2.5x leverage) or Red Rock (owned property, 4.1x), the drivers of forecast differences are diffuse and operational: visitation, hold, labour, one development at a time. At PENN the drivers are a mix (relocation ramps, interactive losses, 5.3x to 5.7x lease-adjusted leverage). Bally's sits at the far end: closer to Caesars' operating company in 2014 or Station Casinos in 2008, where the forecast was the capital structure. That is why the first set's precedent list (Station, CEOC, Revel) is the right one and PENN's Aurora ramp is only a partial comparator.

5. For the forecasts. Test the three dated drivers for size and timing only; they will not surprise in direction. Spend the predictability work on Chicago's opening quarter and first two quarters of receipts, and on the refinancing terms. Treat every other operating line as noise within $25m of EBITDA.

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

F1828th of 28

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Control has been centralised in the controlling shareholder, and the finance function has been hollowed out at the worst possible moment. Together these signal preparation for a capital-structure event that will be run by Standard General, not an operating renewal.

  • Soohyung Kim, Standard General's Managing Partner and Chief Investment Officer, moved from non-executive chair (since December 2019) to Executive Chair on 27 January 2026, with an employment agreement. That ended the independence of the nominating committee. Standard General holds 66.8% (April 2026 record date; 67.1% at 28 February 2026; 73.8% stated after the February 2025 merger).

Marcus Glover, CFO from May 2023, was moved to operations in March 2025 and left in October 2025. That makes three CFO changes in under three and a half years, during refinancing.

  • What it signals: a restructuring specialist hired and then lost, a treasurer gone and the controlling owner taking executive control all point to decisions on refinancing, asset sales or recapitalisation being made at Standard General. That raises the odds that terms favour the majority holder (C-63, C-87). One positive: Papanier has 40 years of land-based experience and long relationships with creditors and regulators.
C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

F1828th of 28

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Strategy has changed many times, from regional roll-up (2019-21) to omni-channel (2021-23), to "Bally's 2.0" cost-out (2025), to metropolitan mega-developments funded by selling real estate. The stated priority has now shifted to preserving capital (Q2 2026 10-Q), but actions still contradict it. The change is poorly articulated and resourced with expensive capital that does not belong to shareholders.

  • The omni-channel thesis failed on the evidence. North America Interactive lost $40m to $60m in 2023, led to a $390.7m impairment in 2024 and a migration onto White Hat and Kambi, and the Sinclair/Bally Sports media tie-up came to little.
  • International interactive was contributed into Intralot (October 2025) for €2.7bn of enterprise value at about 8.5 times forward EBITDA. That is effectively a partial monetisation that keeps 58% control. It is the most coherent strategic move of the period.
  • Development ambition outruns funding: Chicago (minimum $1.34bn, likely over $1.7bn), the Bronx (a $4bn resort; $500m licence; opening 2030), the Las Vegas Tropicana site (beside the A's stadium, due 2028), and Star Entertainment (about 38%).
  • Capital preservation is said but not done. The 10-Q says capital preservation is now the priority, yet Bally's bought Sam's Town Shreveport (August 2026), bought $42.69m of Bally's Intralot shares in H1 2026, and paid the $500m New York licence plus $115m for the Bronx golf-course concession in Q1 2026.
  • Resourcing: sale-leasebacks (Lincoln $700m at an 8% cap rate, adding $56m of rent; Kansas City and Shreveport $395m, adding $32.2m; the Chicago land $250m), GLPI development funding, the WhiteHawk loan at SOFR plus 850, and 11% notes. No equity has been raised.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

F1828th of 28

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Bally's is selling assets, but to raise cash, not to simplify. The operating perimeter is widening and getting more complex, which lowers predictability.

  • Reductions: the Asian interactive business sold to its management (October 2024) for a €30m seven-year note; Tropicana Las Vegas closed (April 2024) and demolished; real estate sold and leased back (Lincoln, Kansas City, Shreveport, the Chicago land); International Interactive moved into Intralot.

C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

F1528th of 28

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Bally's is entering several large new markets at once. Except Chicago, the entries are not sensible for a company with a going-concern warning, and even Chicago has been weakened by the video-terminal decision and by PENN.

  • Chicago (permanent, spring 2027). It is strategically sound: a downtown destination in a large metro, a database of more than 113,000 customers built at the temporary casino, and the structure topped off in July 2026. But Fitch calls the metro saturated and heavily taxed; the temporary casino is below target; the legal terminals erode exclusivity; and the hotel is paused.
  • The Bronx (2030). A $4bn resort, a $500m licence already paid and SOFR-plus-850 pre-construction debt, for a company worth $850m in the market. It is an option bought at a very high premium.
  • Las Vegas: a development option on 35 acres beside the A's stadium, with a $14.6m ground lease and little capital to develop it.
  • Australia (Star): speculative, carries regulatory risk, and Deutsche Bank's credit analyst called any involvement an adverse credit event.
  • Lottery and Evoke: done through the ring-fenced subsidiary; strategically coherent there (UK consolidation), but levered.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

F2828th of 28

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms, and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent?

Judgement: There is no evidence of real price increases in the US. In the UK, prices are being raised in nominal terms to pass the duty through, not to expand margins. Real pricing power is latent only in Chicago and in Rhode Island's monopoly online casino.

  • US casinos: the price levers are promotional reinvestment and payout ratios. The regional promotional environment is rational (Deutsche Bank), but Bally's has to raise reinvestment against competition in Shreveport and Dover. Deliberately cutting unprofitable lower-tier play improves the mix, not the price.
  • US online: none; Bally's is a price-taker.
  • UK (Bally's Intralot B2C): generosity cuts (€25m of the €50m mitigation plan) are a price increase to customers. Revenue still grew 11.6% in constant currency in Q2, the strongest evidence of some pricing power, but the cuts only partly restore the margin lost to the duty, so they do not amount to real price gains.
  • Latent: a downtown Chicago monopoly, now diluted by the video terminals; Rhode Island's monopoly online casino (+23.3% in July 2026).
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

F1828th of 28

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: M&A has stepped up and is poorly disciplined at the parent. The record is of overpaying and then writing down (over $770m of impairments in eighteen months). The best deal is the Intralot combination, and the riskiest is now in the subsidiary (Evoke).

  • Intralot (October 2025): sensible in structure. Bally's received about €1.53bn in cash plus 873.7m shares valued at €1.30, retained 58% control, and took 40% pro forma EBITDA margins with €41m of synergies (€15m in 2026, €25m in 2027). Integration risk is real: the Gamesys and New York culture clash, and a dual CEO.
  • Queen (February 2025): a related-party merger with Standard General's own casinos, part of a deal that bought out public shares at $18.25 funded by $500m of 11% notes ($416m of repurchases). The shares now trade at $13.52.
  • Sam's Town (August 2026): bought while under a going-concern warning; gaming revenue fell 29.1% during the transition.
  • Star (late 2025): debt converted to about 38% equity, plus up to A$300m of convertible notes; a distressed, penalised partner.
  • Evoke, through Bally's Intralot: £243m of equity, about £2.2bn of enterprise value at 6.3 times FY2025 EBITDA, all-share with a cash alternative capped at £117.1m, an £889m second-lien facility at 11%, and £180m of synergies (21% of combined EBITDA, against 5% to 10% for typical deals). Evoke delivered £150m against a £100m target on William Hill, which supports feasibility, but the deal pushes the subsidiary to about 4.5x leverage (Fitch 6.0x).
  • Record: Gamesys bought at a premium in 2021, then $390.7m (2024), $197.5m and $181.6m of impairments.

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

F828th of 28

Judgement: The balance sheet is exhausted. There are no shareholder returns and none are mooted. The only "return" activity is the parent buying shares in its own subsidiary to protect control while under a going-concern warning.

  • The 2025 tender ($416m at $18.25) was a take-private feature funded with 11% debt, not a return of surplus capital.

C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: There are real positives, but they are either far off, partly or wholly owned by others, or small next to the fixed-charge gap.

  1. The permanent Chicago casino: topped off July 2026, targeted for spring 2027. Analysts see stabilised EBITDAR of $200m to $240m ($250m per Deutsche Bank) against about $20m of land rent plus 8.5% on GLPI's $940m (Jefferies puts total extra rent at $132m), so rent coverage of 2.0x to 2.4x.

Bally's economic share is 58% or less after Evoke dilutes it.

  5. The Bronx financing in place ($560m from WhiteHawk), which defers the Bronx funding question.
  6. Standard General's evident commitment: a $18.25 deal, executive control and statements that Bally's will buy Bally's Intralot shares. It makes a support or recapitalisation plausible, though not necessarily on terms that favour minorities.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

F2828th of 28

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Standard General has shown a great deal of appetite for change, but always in the direction of more control for itself. The next change is likely to be a capital-structure solution dictated by Standard General. Whether that crystallises or rescues minority value is the central uncertainty for the equity.

  • Record: a $38 bid (January 2022, terminated); a $15 bid (March 2024, 7.8 times forward EV/EBITDA, called "woefully undervalued" by the activist K&F Growth Capital); the $18.25 merger with Queen (completed February 2025), under which Standard General kept the company listed and took about 74%, now 66.8%; Kim's move to Executive Chair (January 2026); and buying Bally's Intralot shares.
  • K&F (April 2024) demanded that Bally's monetise international interactive and focus on retail margins, which partly happened through the Intralot transaction.
  • Plausible next steps: Standard General injecting equity or debt; a second take-private of the roughly 26% to 33% float, worth about $250m to $300m at today's price; the parent drawing cash from the subsidiary; or a restructuring in which Standard General keeps control. The Station Casinos 2009-11 restructuring and Caesars' 2015 CEOC bankruptcy are precedents for controlling owners keeping the assets while other stakeholders bear the losses.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

F1828th of 28

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Weak, narrow and under-resourced. The genuine positives are real but do not come close to the size of the problem.

  • The positives: North America Interactive's turnaround, the landside conversions, the Intralot structure, UK mitigation and Chicago's topping-off.
  • Why they are insufficient:
  • Together they add perhaps tens of millions of dollars a year to parent EBITDA by 2027. The parent's structural deficit is about $200m a year before capex (Notes, point 2).
  • The largest positive, Chicago, brings new fixed rent with it, and the remaining positives sit largely in the 58%-owned, ring-fenced subsidiary.
  • The resources behind them belong to others (GLPI, WhiteHawk, bondholders at 11% to 13%, Standard General).
  • Leadership has been destabilised in finance just when execution matters most.
C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

F2228th of 28

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: The staircase is thin, back-ended and interleaved with dated negative steps. The most powerful near-term positive is a financing or ownership event, not an operating one. Chicago's opening is the one novel, well-founded operating milestone, but its earnings benefit is weak and slow after rent.

Next 6 months (to about May 2027):

  • Refinancing or covenant reset before the 31 March 2027 waiver expiry. Highly novel and the most valuable. The latest review's "change my mind" trigger is $500m or more refinanced below a 10% blended cost, with maturities beyond 2029 and liquidity-maintenance covenants removed. Founding: weak to medium, given the going-concern note, the GLPI warning and 11% to 13% secondary yields.
  • A Standard General recapitalisation or take-private proposal. Novel. Founding: plausible from history; the terms are the risk.
  • Resolution of Chicago's temporary-licence extension and the dispute over the video terminals and the host agreement (the deadline is 10 December 2026). Novel only as the removal of a negative. Founding: medium.
  • A Tropicana land sale or other monetisation. Medium novelty and medium founding (Mergermarket, 16 September 2026).
  • Bally's Intralot's Q3 results (27 November 2026) and Evoke completion (late 2026 or early 2027): evidence of UK mitigation and scale. Medium novelty; founded on shareholder approval (99.63%) and the financing, but it adds leverage.
  • A permanent CFO appointment. Low novelty; signals the route.
  • Negative steps in the same window: Q3 2026 results (the first full update since the going-concern note), the H2 UK generosity cuts, Sam's Town's transition, and possible liquidated damages.

Months 6 to 18 (about May 2027 to May 2028):

  • Chicago's permanent casino opens (spring 2027) and its first quarters of ramp-up. The most novel operating step. The construction milestone is well founded (topped off July 2026; GLPI has funded $640.7m). Its economics are poorly founded (rent, the terminals, PENN's Aurora, a temporary casino below target, the hotel paused).
  • North America Interactive sustaining profitability; Rhode Island online casino growth. Low novelty.
  • Synergies: the subsidiary's €25m in 2027 and 75% of Evoke's £180m in the first year. Medium novelty and medium founding.
  • Negative steps in the same window: the UK betting duty at 25% (April 2027); Ohio's exit (mid-2027); the Illinois lottery expiry (October 2027); Chicago's rent starting.

Months 18 to 36 (about May 2028 to late 2029):

  • Chicago stabilises at $200m to $250m of EBITDAR (2.0x to 2.4x rent coverage).
  • The A's stadium opens (2028), with possible Las Vegas development or a sale.
  • The subsidiary deleverages towards 2.5x (Deutsche Bank: FY2029).
  • Bronx construction progresses towards a 2030 opening.

Novelty in this window is medium to high, but the founding depends entirely on the capital structure surviving to 2028 without severe dilution.

Narrative context:

  • Price and momentum: $13.52, down 27.4% over twelve months, technical momentum D, relative-strength percentile 25.
  • Sector: the gambling sector is Underweight and Fragility Double Underweight in the FfF.
  • Sell-side: 0% buy ratings.
  • What it means: the market narrative is one of distress. Any staircase must first get past the refinancing question.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

F3027th of 28

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

F3027th of 28

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The industry is structurally sound for a well-capitalised incumbent. Regional casino markets are licence-capped local oligopolies with rational, return-focused competitors, and UK online is consolidating under tax pressure towards scaled operators like Bally's subsidiary. The problem is Bally's position in the value chain, not the industry. Bally's is an operator squeezed between a landlord that captures the property economics (EBITDA margins above 80%, 8.5% yields with escalators, three master leases), suppliers with pricing power, lenders charging 11% to 13%, and fiscal authorities treating gaming as a revenue source. Its competitive strengths are local and regulatory (Rhode Island, Chicago exclusivity). They are vulnerable to politics, as Chicago's video-terminal decision showed, and to rivals' capital spending, as PENN in Chicagoland showed. Where competitors are well capitalised, the value chain rewards reinvestment capacity, which Bally's no longer has; historical precedent (Station 2009, Caesars' operating company 2015, Revel) shows how that ends for leveraged operators. The subsidiary is on the right side of UK consolidation, but Bally's owns 58% of that, and the parent cannot easily reach the cash.

For the Consumer: Assume Bally's keeps local share where it is protected (Rhode Island, downtown Chicago before the terminals mature), loses share slowly where it faces capitalised rivals (Missouri, Atlantic City, Shreveport, Kansas City), does not gain share in US online, and that value created at Chicago accrues first to GLPI.

Value chain dynamics

C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?F3526th of 28full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

F3528th of 28

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: On balance intermediaries hinder Bally's.

  • US online: Bally's depends on app stores, paid search (UK cost per acquired customer has risen to £200-£300 from £100-£120) and affiliates. Its loyalty database (over 12m Bally Rewards members, against PENN's 32m) is smaller than peers'. The Sinclair media partnership (warrants on 8.2m shares) did not deliver a durable channel.
  • Land-based: Bally's sells directly; the hindrance is physical access (Rhode Island bridge works, Chicago parking).
  • Capital as intermediary: GLPI and private lenders act as gatekeepers to growth; GLPI funding was reportedly postponed to late 2027 (Jefferies).
  • The bright spot: Bally's International Interactive spends 10 percentage points less of revenue on marketing than its peers, with sticky, mostly female bingo-led cohorts. This support is moderately robust.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

F2528th of 28

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Competitors are behaving in a disciplined but capital-intensive way that hurts Bally's, and Bally's position against them is fragile because it cannot match their reinvestment.

  • PENN: over $500m in Chicagoland, the Columbus tower ($100m), a Council Bluffs relocation (2028), lease-adjusted leverage of 5.3x to 5.7x, $354m of buybacks in 2025.
  • Boyd: 2.5x net leverage, ongoing property upgrades, sold Sam's Town to Bally's.
  • Red Rock: a $385m Durango expansion and $296.9m of shareholder returns in 2025.
  • Caesars: removing about $35m of fixed digital marketing in H2 2026 and $20m in 2027, which strengthens its digital margins.
  • Online leaders: spending heavily.
  • Bally's position: the capital gap compounds; each competitor's refresh lowers Bally's share in that market, which lowers EBITDAR, which raises leverage, which further limits reinvestment (C-47).
C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?F1528th of 28full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

F2228th of 28

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Every link in the chain is being pushed in a direction that moves value away from Bally's.

  • Demand: younger and lower-income customers are moving to mobile, prediction markets and lower spend.
  • Competition: capitalised rivals are relocating and refreshing; online leaders are subsidising.
  • Regulators and politicians: treating gaming as a fiscal resource (UK, Chicago, state taxes).
  • Suppliers: slot makers are consolidating; content owners such as Evolution have pricing power; AI-driven trading (Kambi: 60% of bets traded by AI) favours scale.
  • Landlords: REITs are gaining power over development (GLPI owns all improvements, takes 8.5% yields, has a right of first refusal on New York and controls Chicago funding timing).
  • Lenders: private credit has replaced public markets at 11% to 13%.

Bally's sits between strengthening suppliers of capital and real estate and weakening, taxed demand, with no proprietary technology in US online.

Industry structure

Judgement30C-2438C-2545C-2638C-2733C-2845C-2938C-3135C-3528C-3640
Group judgementGroup judgement: Industry and Value Chain (Layer 1)F3027th of 28full answer under 3 Value chain dynamics and industry structure
C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

F3826th of 28

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: It depends on the arena: strong locally, mid-table nationally, sub-scale online in North America, and number two in UK online casino through the subsidiary. The arenas that carry the parent's earnings and debt (US land-based) are where it is mid-table and capital-poor.

  • US regional casinos: 20 casinos in 11 states, with about $1.5bn of revenue in FY2025 and about $1.58bn of consensus for FY2026. That is well behind PENN (retail about $1.5bn a quarter), Caesars' regional business and Boyd. Bally's claims to be the third-largest regional network by property count. Locally it is number one in Rhode Island (both of the state's casinos, plus exclusive online casino), holds downtown Chicago exclusivity, and runs several sub-scale properties (Atlantic City, Evansville, Dover, Kansas City).
  • US online: low single-digit share (about 2% in Pennsylvania). FanDuel (31.2%) and DraftKings (30.1%) lead; BetMGM holds 13%.
  • UK online, through Bally's Intralot B2C: number two in online casino, with a stable 14% to 15% share since 2020. With Evoke it would be number two in UK iGaming and number four in UK online sports betting.
  • US lottery technology, through Bally's Intralot B2B: number three, with 10 of 46 state contracts against Brightstar's 26.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

F4526th of 28

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Every market Bally's competes in is concentrated. The concentration helps where Bally's is the local incumbent and hurts where it is a sub-scale challenger (US online).

  • US online: the top five hold more than 85% (84.6% of trailing iGaming revenue per Deutsche Bank). The distribution is very steep: FanDuel and DraftKings hold about 60% to 65% between them, against low single digits for Bally's.
  • UK online: the top five held 69% in 2024 (up from 51% in 2014); the top three about 50%; the rest is spread over about 200 companies and 2,000 brands, so it is steep below the top five and tax-driven consolidation is steepening it.
  • US regional land-based: concentrated locally because licences are capped (usually one to four operators per market); nationally the top five are a minority, but competition is local.
  • US lottery technology: effectively three suppliers.
  • Slot supply: the top three hold about 75%.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

F3825th of 28

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Land-based shares are stable except when a rival relocates or new supply opens, and those disruptions are now hitting Bally's markets (Chicagoland, Shreveport, Dover, New York). Online shares are unstable: new entrants have taken material share in US sports (Fanatics) and are taking attention through prediction markets. In the UK, share is moving to scale, which favours the subsidiary.

  • Land-based: PENN's Aurora relocation nearly doubled its volumes; Bally's lost 30 basis points in Missouri; Sam's Town's revenue fell 29% during the change of owner.
  • US online: Fanatics went from 6.1% to 9.5% in four months; prediction markets went from under 1% to 16% of sports bettors' active users while sportsbooks fell from 76% to 64% within twelve months (Entain digest); Hard Rock and Rush Street took iGaming share in Michigan and New Jersey.
  • UK: Tier 1 gained 7 percentage points since 2021, and Bally's International Interactive's share has been stable at 14% to 15%.
  • Lottery: renewal rates of 89% to 97%, though price resets are under way (Montana renewed at 5% of stakes, from 8%).
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

F3327th of 28

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Share is consolidating towards the scaled and the well-capitalised, not simply the largest. Bally's is among the advantaged only in UK online, through the ring-fenced subsidiary. In US land-based and US online it is on the losing side, because consolidation is driven by reinvestment capacity, which it lacks.

  • US online: share is going to the operators with databases and large budgets (FanDuel, DraftKings), or with cross-subsidies (Fanatics' merchandise loyalty, Caesars' rewards database). Sub-scale players such as Rush Street, PENN Interactive (2.2%) and Bally's are takeover targets (Bernstein).
  • US land-based: "advantaged" means modern, recently relocated or refreshed properties and owned real estate (PENN's Aurora, Joliet and Columbus tower; Boyd owns 90% of its properties; Red Rock's Durango targets returns over 20%). Deutsche Bank: Bally's leverage "prevents [it] from investing in legacy property maintenance to defend market share."
  • UK online: the duty is a tax on lack of scale. The subsidiary, with low marketing intensity and a proprietary data platform (Vitruvian), is among the advantaged, and Evoke would add scale. The sector digest calls this story "Big; Early for Bally's Intralot".
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

F4522nd of 28

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Attitudes depend on the arena.

  • US regional: rational and focused on margin and returns, and benign on promotions (Deutsche Bank). Boyd and Red Rock return cash and do not start price wars. Promotional reinvestment has not historically collapsed when volumes fell, apart from local spikes. Operators guard margin by cutting low-tier play and reinvestment; Bally's does the same.
  • US online: volume first and willing to lose money. The leaders' marketing rose 40% to 60% in 2026; FanDuel committed $300m to its prediction product. Bally's cannot compete on these terms, and its shift to profitability means it has conceded volume.
  • UK online: large operators protect margin by cutting generosity and costs. During consolidation some are buying volume (Flutter raising generosity in H2 2026), so prices in the form of bonuses rise when a leader wants share.

For the Consumer: The land-based structure supports Bally's margins in benign conditions. The risk is not a price war but competitors' capital spending (C-29).

C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

F3822nd of 28

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Regional attitudes are stable. Fifteen years of post-2008 discipline have been reinforced by REIT ownership and listed-company return targets, with no sign of O'Leary-style volatility. The realistic "shark attack" is not on price but on capacity and product: well-capitalised rivals upgrading facilities in Bally's markets while Bally's cannot answer.

  • That attack is already visible: PENN's Chicagoland relocations, Boyd's refresh programme, Resorts World Queens, and new supply in Shreveport and Dover.
  • US online is unstable: spending has stepped up in 2026 (the World Cup, prediction-market defence).
  • UK attitude is in flux: consolidation is in progress, and a dominant player (Flutter) can raise generosity tactically.
  • Bally's weakness is public: a going-concern warning and a GLPI warning. Competitors' best response is to target Bally's high-value players with hosts and promotions, the pattern already seen when relationship-marketing staff left Atlantic City in 2024 and high-value retention dropped.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

F3527th of 28

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Supportive in land-based gaming for a healthy incumbent; unsupportive in US online; supportive in UK online for scaled operators. For Bally's in particular the support is fragile, because the value it creates is captured by others in the value chain and by its capital providers.

  • Land-based: licence caps protect incumbents. But the landlords capture the property economics (GLPI earns EBITDA margins above 80%; rents rise with escalators), slot suppliers earn 40% to 50% EBITDA margins, and lenders charge 11% to 13%, leaving the operator mid-to-high-20s EBITDAR margins that become low single-digit margins after rent, depreciation and interest.
  • Where the support breaks:
  • Political: the Chicago video-terminal decision shows a licensed exclusivity can be diluted by local politics.
  • Competitive: relocations by rivals.
  • Financial: a master-lease default would cross-default.
  • UK online: supportive through tax-driven consolidation, but fragile to further tax rises and offshore leakage.
C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?F2828th of 28full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

F4027th of 28

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: A moderate threat, rising slowly.

  • US: alternatives for discretionary entertainment spending (dining out, travel, streaming, prediction markets, social casino) gain in a squeeze on low- and middle-income households. Experts note regional customers are increasingly playing on their phones across state borders. Younger cohorts come to gambling through digital channels, so ageing floors face slow erosion.
  • Countervailing: regional casinos are local, frequent, drive-to entertainment that historically fell materially only in the financial crisis and COVID-19 (Morgan Stanley). Locals substitute away from expensive Las Vegas trips towards drive-to visits.
  • UK: unlicensed sites are the main substitute, and duty pass-through makes them more attractive.
