4a

Business quality

F2828th of 288 answers
Judgement28C-4132C-4225C-4340C-4432C-4530C-4625C-4718
Group judgement

Company: Competitive advantage

F2828th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Bally's has advantages, but they are in the wrong places for its common equity. The strongest durable moat in the group, Rhode Island's casino and online-casino monopoly to 2043, is real but small and comes with dividend restrictions while leverage exceeds 5.5x. The most valuable growth moat, Bally's International Interactive's data-led, low-marketing UK business, now widening as the 40% duty culls sub-scale rivals, sits inside a ring-fenced subsidiary that Bally's owns 58% of and cannot freely draw cash from. The flagship development moat, downtown Chicago exclusivity, is being diluted by video terminals before the permanent casino opens. In the remaining regional estate and in US online, Bally's has no demonstrable advantage and is losing ground to better-capitalised peers, and the mechanical forces (rent escalators, cost of capital, competitors' reinvestment) work against it with each period. Pricing power is demonstrated only as UK tax pass-through, and that is untested against H2 2026 player behaviour.

For the Consumer: Do not model margin expansion from competitive advantage at the parent. Model Rhode Island as a stable floor, Chicago with a terminal-dilution haircut, and UK online share gains at the subsidiary with only partial economic look-through.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

F3227th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Bally's has one strong, durable moat (Rhode Island), one valuable but narrowing moat (Chicago exclusivity), a strong moat it owns 58% of (Bally's International Interactive's UK data and marketing efficiency), a moderate moat in lottery switching costs (also inside the subsidiary), and no moat in US online or in most of its regional properties. Its soft advantages (culture, governance, execution) are negative.

  1. Regulatory: Rhode Island. Bally's owns both Rhode Island casinos and is the sole provider of online slots and table games for 20 years, to 2043, under the Marc A. Crisafulli Act structure with Allwyn and Brightstar. Strong and stable, though the regulatory agreement restricts payouts while leverage exceeds 5.5x and bars expansion into neighbouring states. The Washington Bridge works are a temporary drag (about $2m a quarter). Under 20% of rated slot play comes from customers closer to Boston competitors. The trend is stable.
  2. Regulatory: Chicago downtown exclusivity under the host agreement. Valuable, but narrowing: the video terminals have been legalised, and the dispute over whether that breaches the agreement is unresolved.
  3. Customer and know-how: Bally's International Interactive (UK). Marketing about 10 percentage points below peers' share of revenue (11% against Evoke's 23%); bingo-led female cohorts (about 60% of customers); a 43% standalone EBITDA margin before the duty; the proprietary Vitruvian data platform. Strong, and widening in relative terms as the duty forces sub-scale rivals out. Owned 58%.
  4. Switching costs: Intralot's lottery technology. A 16-year average remaining contract term, 89% renewals since 2006 and €1.4bn of backlog. Moderate and narrowing at re-tender (Ohio lost, Montana repriced, Illinois due October 2027). Owned 58%.
  5. Scale: weak (C-42).
  6. Soft advantages: negative (Management group).
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

F2528th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Bally's is mid-sized and its scale confers little advantage. Its revenue ($2.66bn in FY2025; consensus $3.16bn for FY2026 including Intralot) is about a third of PENN's, under a third of Caesars', comparable to Boyd's, a fifth of Flutter's ($16.4bn) and under half of Entain's (£5.3bn). Scale gives no purchasing advantage with concentrated slot suppliers or content owners, no capital-cost advantage (its cost of capital is the sector's highest), and no online advantage in North America (low single-digit share). The only scale that matters is the subsidiary's UK online scale (number two in online casino), which lets it absorb the duty better than sub-scale rivals. It had about 10,000 employees at end-2024.

C-43How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?F4026th of 28full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

F3228th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Inferiority is disproved in UK online and in the newly built properties, but not disproved, and in places shown, in the older regional estate and in US online.

  • Not inferior:
  • UK online: growth of 10.5% to 11.6% in constant currency, ahead of local competitors, without extra marketing, at an all-time high in net gaming revenue.
  • Baton Rouge and Marquette after moving onto land: very large gains in gaming revenue.
  • Chicago's permanent casino should be a new, superior downtown product.
  • Rhode Island: a monopoly product.
  • Inferior or unproven:
  • US online: experts describe Bally's as "significantly behind DraftKings and FanDuel", with low single-digit share and "no differentiation" (a Light & Wonder vice-president, October 2026).
  • Older properties: Bally's has "lost customer loyalty partly due to not keeping up with technology" (former competitor); its leverage prevents maintenance spending (Deutsche Bank); Atlantic City is losing to larger operators; app downloads grew 12% against Boyd's 146% (August 2026).
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

F3027th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: The only demonstrated pricing power is in UK online, where generosity cuts are passing the duty to players while revenue still grows. Elsewhere there is no evidence that Bally's has applied pricing power.

  • UK evidence: of the Q2 2026 duty hit of €34.0m (Bally's Intralot reporting; $39m on Bally's reporting), 65% was offset (€11.3m from costs, €10.1m from growth). The planned €50m of mitigation includes €25m from cutting generosity. Constant-currency growth accelerated from 10.5% in Q1 to 11.6% in Q2. The real test, how players respond to the H2 2026 cuts, is not yet in the data.
  • US land-based: promotions are a competitive tool. Bally's has had to raise reinvestment in Shreveport and Dover, and Deutsche Bank's model has Chicago's slot promotional spend at 13.5% of gaming revenue.
  • US online: a price-taker.
  • Rhode Island monopoly online casino: latent.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

F2528th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto?

Judgement: It does not. There is neither a stated nor a de facto policy of sharing scale economies with customers. Strategy and messaging focus on cost-out, margin protection and capital preservation. The UK generosity cuts are the opposite of sharing, though they are tax-forced, and the voluntary trimming of lower-tier players is a margin move. Bally's lacks the scale economies to share in the first place (C-42).

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

F1828th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: The mechanical forces mostly work against Bally's. Each period that passes worsens its competitive position and financial outputs against peers unless a large discrete event (Chicago's ramp-up, refinancing or recapitalisation) intervenes.

  • Against Bally's:
  • Rent escalators on three master leases; interest at 11% to 13% on refinancing; peers refreshing properties while Bally's cannot.
  • A self-reinforcing loop: lower investment leads to share loss, then lower EBITDAR, higher leverage and higher cost of capital, and lower investment again.
  • Rising US online acquisition costs.
  • UK duty steps (April 2027).
  • Lottery re-tenders repricing.
  • For Bally's:
  • The Rhode Island monopoly ages well, to 2043.
  • UK duty-driven consolidation transfers share to Bally's International Interactive, partly mechanically.
  • Lottery contracts run long (a 16-year average term).
  • The North America Interactive cost base is now variable, so operating leverage there turns positive as revenue grows.

The pro-Bally's forces are concentrated in the 58%-owned subsidiary.

4b

Management quality

F1228th of 2812 answers
Judgement12C-5535C-5615C-5728C-57b18C-588C-5912C-6015C-6118C-6215C-638C-645
Group judgement

Company: Management

F1228th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Management is the binding weakness behind the financial weakness, and governance determines who bears the outcome. The CEO is a capable digital operator who delivered the North America Interactive turnaround and built the UK franchise that is now the group's best asset. But the parent's strategy has been set by a controlling shareholder pursuing very large, debt- and lease-funded ambitions with little regard for balance-sheet resilience. The result is a record of missed guidance, late projects, over $770m of impairments, discretionary pay and a finance function hollowed out at the moment of greatest need. Governance offers minorities little protection: Standard General holds about 67%, has bid three times, has injected its own assets in a related-party merger financed with 11% debt, now holds the executive chair, and will design the coming capital-structure solution.

For the Consumer: Weight forecasts that depend on management delivery (the Chicago timeline and costs, synergies, cost-outs) towards slippage, and assume any recapitalisation is priced to suit the controlling shareholder. Watch the identity and background of the permanent CFO as the clearest signal of Standard General's intended route (refinancing, recapitalisation or restructuring).

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

F3528th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Ambition is very large, larger than any regional peer's relative to size, but it is not anchored to the balance sheet. That makes it a source of fragility rather than a force for outperformance.

  • The ambition: Bally's has simultaneously pursued a downtown Chicago resort (a minimum of $1.34bn, probably above $1.7bn), a $4bn Bronx resort, a Las Vegas Strip redevelopment beside the A's stadium, an Australian casino stake (Star), a global lottery and iGaming group (Intralot, then Evoke), and US online. The Leadership digest notes these commitments "total over five billion dollars against a company generating negative returns on invested capital and operating near covenant violation thresholds."
  • Peers: PENN, Boyd and Red Rock pursue growth through one or two funded projects at a time, and keep buying back shares.
  • How it shows: the ambition is pushing (licences won, structures topped off), but much of it is opportunistic deal-making that Standard General drives, rather than out-competing on product.
C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

F1528th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: The strategy is bold but not smart. It works only in benign credit and regulatory conditions, and it did not widen the moat at the parent.

  • Fragile by design: it depends on cheap capital (sale-leasebacks, high-yield debt, landlord funding) and on regulators keeping their side of the bargain (Chicago). It has no buffer for higher rates, a consumer slowdown or adverse tax changes, which is exactly today's environment.
  • The omni-channel thesis failed: North America Interactive impairments, Sinclair, and a sub-scale online share.
  • The best strategic move was combining the international interactive business with Intralot, a scale play in UK online that captures the tax-driven consolidation. It sits in a subsidiary and has been followed by a levered Evoke bid.
  • Priorities are not set by choices: the stated pivot to capital preservation (Q2 2026 10-Q) has not changed behaviour (Sam's Town, buying Bally's Intralot shares, the Bronx).
C-57

How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

F2828th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Robeson Reeves is a capable digital operator with a strong record before Bally's, but his record as Bally's CEO is weak, and the decisive capital decisions appear to be Standard General's. He is not an "animal CEO", and with a dual role he is stretched.

  • Before Bally's: at Gamesys he rose to COO (2015) and led its growth, with revenue going from £340m (2018) to £709m (2020), leverage from 3.8x to 1.1x and cash conversion of 94%, and he integrated Jackpotjoy (2019).
  • At Bally's (CEO since 31 March 2023):
  • For him: he called interactive results "unacceptable" and fixed North America Interactive, which was profitable by Q2 2026; he did the Intralot deal and kept UK growth going.
  • Against him:
    • Guidance: cut in November 2023 ($35m off EBITDAR at the midpoint; $100m off revenue); 2024 guidance below consensus.
    • Chicago: the temporary casino ran months late.
    • Strategy: a "TikTok-style" betting vision that failed (expert); a culture clash after Gamesys; no land-based casino experience in a company that is about 72% land-based.
    • Credibility: he said in 2024 that no assets were non-core, then carved out international interactive.
    • Dual role: since November 2025 he has also been Group CEO of Bally's Intralot.
  • Pay: $4.54m in FY2023, a period of losses; an $871,046 discretionary award for completing the Intralot deal.

How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

How clear, straightforward and helpful is the company's communication, including on the strategy and the driver transmission mechanism?

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

F828th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: The evidence shows that the controlling shareholder comes first, management second and minority shareholders third. Governance is not prudent. There is little scaffolding beyond the legal minimum, and the controlled-company structure lets Standard General set the terms of every capital-structure decision. The reason is structural: Standard General owns about 67% (73.8% after the 2025 merger), controls the parent and, through it, the subsidiary, and its interests (control, the assets, option value) can diverge from those of minorities in a distressed recapitalisation.

  • Control and conflicted transactions:
  • Bids at $38 (2022), $15 (2024; called "woefully undervalued" by K&F) and $18.25 (2025). The last was combined with Standard General contributing its own Queen casinos for 30.5m Bally's shares, and was funded with $500m of 11% notes that Bally's shareholders now carry (Deutsche Bank: about -$20m a year of cash flow).
  • Kim became Executive Chair (January 2026), ending the nominating committee's independence; there is no lead independent director.
  • The board has four independent and three non-independent directors.
  • The controlled-company designation allows NYSE exemptions, which Bally's says it does not currently use.
  • Pay versus performance:
  • The 2023 EBITDA target of $557m was abandoned and 100% of PSUs paid anyway.
  • Discretionary transaction bonuses.
  • Pay rose or held while shareholder returns fell.
  • The Leadership digest places Bally's in the bottom percentile on alignment of pay with performance.
  • Controls: material weaknesses (tax; FX accounting under ASC 830), restatements, late filings, a Pomerantz investigation and a reported SEC inquiry.
  • Scaffolding that exists:
  • An independent audit committee (chaired by Cheryl Harris; 12 meetings in FY2024) and a fully independent compensation committee.
  • Clawback policies (2023).
  • No hedging or pledging allowed.
  • A CEO shareholding guideline of five times salary.
  • The special committee raised the price from $15 to $18.25.
  • Insider dealing: no open-market buying by executives (Form 4s show grants and Standard General-related filings only).
  • The subsidiary: related-party expenses of €24.23m in 2025, and payables to the parent rising, which is a channel by which the parent can draw cash from the subsidiary.
C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

F528th of 28

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

Judgement: It is not. The capital-allocation record is among the worst in the sector.

  • Write-downs: over $770m of impairments in eighteen months.
  • Gamesys: bought at a premium (2021), then partly written down; its technology was abandoned (BetWorks).
  • Sale-leasebacks: at cap rates around 8% to 8.5% (Kansas City and Shreveport at 12.3 times rent), turning real estate into permanently higher fixed charges.
  • The 2025 tender: $416m at $18.25, funded with 11% debt, against $13.52 today.
  • The Bronx licence: $500m, with no cash flow before 2030.
  • Star: a distressed stake.
  • Sam's Town: bought under a going-concern warning.
  • Bally's Intralot shares: $42.69m bought in H1 2026 under the same warning.
  • Returns to shareholders: none since 2021; suspended.
  • The one value-crystallising move was contributing the international interactive business to Intralot at about 8.5 times forward EBITDA.

Financial profile

F628th of 288 answers
Judgement6C-496C-5015C-515C-524C-5315C-545C-763
Group judgement

Company: Economic strength

F628th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Economic strength is the weakest part of the case. Bally's has a positive operating business (about $730m of consolidated segment EBITDAR) inside a capital structure that consumes all of it. Rent (about $254m, rising by about $100m to $132m when Chicago opens), interest (about $476m, rising with refinancing and the Bronx loan) and depreciation and amortisation (about $380m to $420m) exceed EBITDAR, so EPS is deeply negative through at least FY2028 on consensus (-$8.11, -$5.41, -$5.26). Free cash flow is negative (FY2026 consensus -$480m). Returns on capital are negative, with over $770m of impairments in eighteen months. Margins are below peers and falling for dated, fixed reasons. The earnings base is very unclean.

The ring-fence makes it worse for common equity. On my split, the parent alone earns about $355m of EBITDAR against about $554m of rent and interest, so it depends on asset sales, landlord and private-credit funding, and Standard General. The equity is about eight times geared to enterprise value: small operating changes become very large equity outcomes, and the outcome is set by financing, not operations.

For the Consumer: Build the model in two layers: a parent model (Casinos & Resorts, North America Interactive, corporate, GLPI rent, parent debt) and a 58% look-through of Bally's Intralot, with dividends and intercompany flows explicit. Make the capital-structure outcome an explicit scenario. Treat consensus EBITDA growth for FY2027 to FY2028 (+11.7% and +16.6%) as at risk from Chicago's rent timing and the ramp-up, and consensus interest as too low if refinancing happens at today's market levels.

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

F628th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: There is no positive earnings per share to grow. Bally's has reported losses every year since FY2020. Revenue growth is acquisition-driven, and the consensus "improvement" is a narrowing of losses that rests on Chicago and on interest cost not rising, neither of which is secure. The loss-making profile is durable at least through FY2028 on consensus.

  • History: reported basic EPS was +$1.46 (FY2019), -$3.51 (FY2023), -$11.71 (FY2024) and -$14.45 (FY2025). On the brokers' basis it was -$11.72 (FY2024) and -$11.59 (FY2025). Revenue grew +8.6% (FY2023, acquisitions), +0.1% (FY2024) and +8.4% (FY2025, Queen plus one quarter of Intralot).
  • Consensus (FactSet median, five brokers): EPS of -$8.11 (FY2026), -$5.41 (FY2027) and -$5.26 (FY2028) on revenue growth of +19.0%, +8.4% and +8.1%. Most FY2026 revenue growth is consolidation (a full year of Intralot, plus Sam's Town); organic growth is low single digits.
  • Why losses persist: Interest (FY2025 net $401m, now about $476m annualised), depreciation and amortisation ($379m to $419m on consensus) and rent together exceed EBITDAR. Each 100 basis points on about $3bn of refinanced parent debt costs about $30m, about $0.50 per share on 60m shares (calculated).
  • Direction of estimates: FY2027 EPS was cut 24% over three months and 140% over twelve months (FactSet). The consensus path is still deteriorating.

For the Consumer: Do not model EPS as a growth series. Model the bridge (EBITDAR, then rent, then depreciation and amortisation, then interest, then tax, then minorities, then share count) and treat EPS as an output with a wide, downward-skewed range.

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

F1528th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: There is scope in principle (Chicago, landside conversions, property refreshes, North America Interactive), but no capital to deploy. The returns on the largest project, after GLPI's rent, are modest. The fair multiple is therefore low: a sub-peer EV/EBITDAR multiple with no growth premium.

  • Chicago economics (my inference; ranges because the temporary casino's EBITDAR is not disclosed separately):
  • Stabilised EBITDAR: $200m to $250m (analysts; Deutsche Bank $250m).
  • Less the temporary casino's EBITDAR that it replaces (undisclosed, and below targets).
  • Less new rent of about $100m (8.5% on $940m plus $20m of land rent) to $132m (Jefferies' total rent increase).
  • Net uplift to EBITDA after rent at stabilisation (2028 at the earliest): about $30m to $110m.
  • Bally's own capital: the $1.34bn minimum, less GLPI's $940m, is $400m or more; if costs reach $1.7bn, about $760m; the Leadership digest cites $450m of additional developer capital.
  • Pre-tax return on Bally's own capital: roughly 5% to 25%, with a mid-point near 10% and execution risk on both sides. That is above the cost of Bally's marginal debt (11% to 13%) only in the better half of outcomes.
  • Landside conversions: high return (Baton Rouge), but done.
  • Property refreshes: probably high return, but unfunded.
  • The Bronx: $4bn for a 2030 opening. The return cannot be estimated; carry it at cost-to-date as an option.
  • North America Interactive: high incremental return on marketing only if Bally's had scale; at low single-digit share, spending would be value-destroying (the Wisdom Library entry "growth funded by incentives cannot fund margin").
C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

F528th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Returns are negative to negligible on every measure, and the record of new investment is value-destroying.

  • Return on invested capital: "negative to low single digits historically" (Jefferies); -2.90% in FY2024 (Jefferies). Red Rock's was 9.6% in 2025, and the sector average is about 11.5%.
  • EBIT margin (FactSet adjusted): 1.1% in FY2025 and 2.4% in FY2024. Consensus EBIT of $115m, $119m and $177m for FY2026 to FY2028 against invested capital of roughly $10bn (equity $2.1bn, net debt about $4.4bn, minorities $1.5bn, capitalised leases about $2bn; calculated) gives a pre-tax return of about 1% to 2%.
  • Return on new invested capital: demonstrably negative. Impairments total over $770m in eighteen months ($390.7m on North America Interactive in FY2024; $197.5m in Q4 2024 and $181.6m in late 2025 on the B2B segment; $18.1m after Q4 2023). The Gamesys acquisition (2021) was written down, and the $18.25 buyout and tender preceded a fall to $13.52.

C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

F428th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Conversion is not meaningful because both net income and free cash flow are negative, and free cash flow is deeply negative and worsening into FY2026. Working capital has turned from a source of cash into a use, as supplier financing unwinds and receivables rise.

  • Free cash flow (FactSet: operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex): -$141m (FY2022), -$123m (FY2023), -$85.8m (FY2024), -$275m (FY2025).
  • Consensus free cash flow: -$480m (FY2026), -$131m (FY2027), +$44.4m (FY2028). Consensus operating cash flow: -$250m, +$135m, +$207m.
  • 2026 so far: operating cash flow was -$145.0m in Q1 and -$265.9m for H1, after $98.9m of upfront licence fees. Capex including intangibles was $611m in H1 2026 (including the $500m New York licence), partly offset by $274.0m of GLPI reimbursements for Chicago.
  • Working capital: receivables plus inventories less payables went from $6.4m (Q4 2024) to $134m (Q1 2026) and $83.0m (Q2 2026). Deferred-payable trade finance fell from $47.0m to $27.7m, a cash outflow. Receivables are rising, so customers are paying more slowly, while payables fall, so Bally's pays suppliers faster. That is a sign of weakening bargaining power and of suppliers tightening terms on a distressed counterparty.
  • Prepaid rent: a $222.7m balance at June 2026 (an asset linked to GLPI funding) and a $90.3m construction receivable from GLPI. These timing items can swing reported cash flow.

For the Consumer: Model cash flow from the parent and subsidiary separately. Consolidated operating cash flow includes Bally's Intralot's €133.5m of H1 2026 operating cash flow, which is trapped in the ring-fenced subsidiary. Parent-only operating cash flow in H1 2026 was therefore materially worse than -$266m (inference).

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

F528th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: The earnings base is very unclean. The gap between adjusted and reported earnings is enormous and recurring, "one-off" items recur, and there are several non-obvious leakages to minorities, tax and the controlling shareholder. Material weaknesses in controls lower confidence in the reported numbers themselves.

  • Size of the gap:
  • Q2 2026: adjusted EBITDA $124.0m against a net loss of $164.0m, a $288m gap.
  • FY2025: brokers'-basis EBITDA $504m against FactSet operating EBITDA $344m (+46.5%).
  • EPS on the brokers' basis: -$11.59, against reported -$14.45.
  • Recurring add-backs:
  • Transaction costs: $21.3m in Q2 2026, $14.0m plus $8.8m (merger and Intralot) in Q1.
  • Development costs: $20.3m excluded in Q2.
  • Tropicana closure costs: $6.2m in Q1.
  • Restructuring: every year since 2023.
  • Flattering items:
  • A $105.8m gain on the Lincoln sale-leaseback in Q1 2026. Without it Q1 operating income would have been a loss of about $14m.
  • A $21.1m foreign-exchange gain in Q1.
  • Capitalised development at Bally's Intralot, including twenty-year amortisation of central systems.
  • Leakage:
  • Tax: the reported rate is negative (-7.2% in FY2025), meaning cash tax is paid in profitable jurisdictions (the UK and Greece) while US losses create no usable shield.
  • Minorities: 42% of Bally's Intralot's profits belong to others once it is profitable; minorities were $1,525m at refresh.
  • Intercompany: payables from the subsidiary rose to €30.31m.
  • Related parties: the Queen transaction; Standard General.
  • Controls:
  • A material weakness in tax accounting (Q2 2026).
  • Ineffective disclosure controls (31 March 2026).
  • Earlier weaknesses in FX accounting (ASC 830).
  • Restatements: the 2021-22 financials and 2022 cash balances.
  • A reported SEC inquiry (Zacks; verify).
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

F328th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Financial leverage is extreme and uncomfortable. It is the defining feature of the case. It turns small operating changes into large equity-value changes and puts solvency, not earnings, at the centre of the forecast.

  • Coverage:
  • H1 2026 consolidated: EBITDAR covered rent plus interest only 1.05 times; adjusted EBITDA to net interest was 1.08 times.
  • Parent (inference): about $355m of EBITDAR against about $254m of rent and about $300m of interest, so fixed charges are not covered.
  • Leverage: rent-adjusted 7.3x to 7.7x (late 2024), 8.2x (early 2025), 11.6x pro forma (November 2025) and 14.0x pro forma for the new leases (Deutsche Bank). FactSet net debt to EBITDA is 18.1x at Q2 2026. Moody's rates the company B2 and projects leverage above 7x for two years.
  • Covenants:
  • The first-lien net leverage covenant on the revolver is waived only to 31 March 2027, conditional on minimum liquidity; management does not project meeting liquidity maintenance for the next twelve months.
  • The interest-rate grid steps up margins above 5.25x consolidated total net leverage.
  • Rhode Island restricts payouts above 5.5x.
  • Debt stack:
  • $1.1bn term loan due 2031 (February 2026).
  • $500m of 11% first-lien notes due October 2028.
  • Legacy unsecured notes, trading at yields up to 12.9%.
  • Revolver: Fitch flagged its October 2026 maturity; whether it was extended is not in the files and must be checked.
  • $400m Bronx term loan at SOFR plus 850 (October 2026), plus a $160m delayed draw.
  • Every 100 basis points on floating debt costs $22.7m.
  • Amplification: enterprise value of $6.8bn against an $852m market capitalisation makes the equity about eight times geared to enterprise value. A 10% change in enterprise value is roughly an 80% change in equity value (calculated). For EPS, the interest line is the swing factor: refinancing about $3bn at 300 basis points more would cost about $90m, or about $1.50 per share (calculated).

Pricing power and operating leverage (and others)

5a

Pricing power

3 answers

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

F2524th of 25

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Price adds almost nothing to Bally's revenue growth over the forecast. In places it is a small negative. The only material price action is a one-off: Bally's Intralot's cuts to UK player generosity in H2 2026, worth about €25m a year, or about 2.5% of B2C revenue. It recovers part of a tax rise rather than expanding margin, and it lands in the 58%-owned subsidiary. In the US casinos, price (promotional reinvestment and hold) is a tool for defending share and gives at best a mix benefit. The Chicago permanent casino will need negative price (heavy free play) to build its database. Rhode Island, the strongest franchise, has no pricing lever at all, because the state sets the economics. My forecasts therefore assume volume and mix only, and hold promotional reinvestment flat to slightly up.

1. Price by business, and what I model:

Business Evidence Price contribution in my base
Casinos & Resorts, core estate Regional promotion is rational (Deutsche Bank, 25 June 2026), but Bally's raised reinvestment against new supply in Shreveport and Dover (Q3 2025 and Q1 2026 releases), and lost 30bp of Missouri share in August 2026. The deliberate pruning of lower-tier and unrated play since 2024 is mix, not price: rated visits +4.3% in Q2 2026, segment revenue +2.0%. Peers' price levers are better: Caesars' regional table hold rose to 20.6% from 19.8% (Q3 2026), and Red Rock converted buffets into high-limit rooms. 0% a year; core revenue growth of 0% (FY2027) to +1% (FY2029 to FY2030) is all volume and mix; promotional reinvestment held flat
Chicago permanent casino Deutsche Bank models gaming promotions at 12% of adjusted gross receipts ($84m on $703m), against an industry estimate of 13.5%. New urban casinos need more while building a database, and the 113,000-member database from the temporary casino is partly a table-heavy, five-mile, younger crowd. Video gaming terminals (Accel holds 17 of the first 44 Chicago approvals) and PENN's Aurora (admissions about doubled) compete for the same convenience slot player. Year-one EBITDAR margin of 20% against a 30% stabilised margin; about 3 to 5 points of that gap is promotional (my inference)
Rhode Island (both casinos and the online casino monopoly to 2043) Bally's acts as agent; the state takes all gaming win and remits a fixed share; the VLT joint venture is 60% IGT; the regulatory agreement bars expansion and restricted payments above 5.5x leverage Zero price lever; stable volume floor
North America Interactive Price-taker. DraftKings cut promotions to 2% of handle in high-tax states (Investor Day, March 2026), which helps the market, but prediction-market keyword bidding has raised acquisition costs by about 40% (Rothschild & Co Redburn, September 2026). Chicago's online sports-betting tax rose to 10.25%, with Illinois' per-wager tax on top. 0%; revenue growth from volume (iGaming-led), margin from operating leverage, not price
Bally's Intralot B2C (UK-led) Q2 2026: 65% of the €34.0m duty hit offset (€11.3m costs, €10.1m growth); UK +11.6% in constant currency, about +13% in July. The generosity cut (€25m of the €50m plan) is a price rise to players. The test is H2 2026 player response, and the AlphaSense claim that players were "preserved" rests on the 4 September call, too early to judge. Precedents: in 2019 William Hill lost 14% of active users but raised revenue per user 13% after the duty rise; the Netherlands' 2025 rise to 34.2% shrank the legal market about 25% as high-value players went offshore. About +2.5 points of B2C revenue growth from H2 2026 to H1 2027 (a one-off step), then 0. It recovers about €25m of a gross duty hit of about €95m to €120m a year. Further duty (General Betting Duty at 25% from April 2027) is not passed through fully
Bally's Intralot B2B (lottery) Re-tenders reprice down: Montana renewed at 5% of stakes from 8%, Ohio lost Negative: about -1% to -2% a year of B2B revenue from repricing (inside my -3% FY2027 and -2% FY2028)

2. How price feeds the margins.

  • B2C: EBITDAR margin goes from 26.6% (Q2 2026) to about 28.5% (H2 2026) and 29% to 30% (FY2027 to FY2030), against 36% to 38% before the duty. Price recovers about a fifth of the lost margin; costs and synergies recover more; about 7 to 8 points are lost permanently.
  • Casinos & Resorts core: margin falls 30bp in FY2027 and 20bp in FY2028 because price cannot offset wage inflation and defensive reinvestment.
  • Group: price contributes roughly +$20m of EBITDA in FY2027 (the UK step) and nothing after. Against $459m of FY2027 EBITDA after rent, that is immaterial to the case.

3. Where pricing could surprise:

  • Up: the UK base holds through the cuts (recreational, female, bingo-led cohorts are less price-sensitive, and the low 11% marketing ratio is a cushion) and sub-scale rivals exit, letting Bally's Intralot cut generosity further. That would add 1 to 2 points to B2C margin.
  • Down: Flutter's higher generosity in H2 2026, or Netherlands-style leakage of high-value players, forces generosity back up. That would cost 2 to 4 points of B2C margin. In Chicago, video terminals spread faster than expected and promotions run at 15% or more.

The downside is the more likely of the two, because the duty steps (April 2026, April 2027) and the political logic of further UK rises (three duty rises in twelve months) are dated and cumulative.

For the Consumer: Model revenue from volume and mix only, with no price-driven margin expansion anywhere at the parent. The one testable pricing event in the next six months is Bally's Intralot's Q3 results (27 November 2026): UK constant-currency growth staying at or above 6% after the generosity cuts would confirm my B2C path, and growth below 3% would put the FY2027 B2C line at risk by $30m to $40m of revenue and $10m to $15m of EBITDAR.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7512P-0112
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

F1228th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Operating leverage is very high, and the opening of a large fixed-rent asset into a ramp-up is about to raise it. A modest change in revenue moves EBIT and pre-tax profit by multiples of itself, producing a very wide range of outcomes.

  • Fixed costs: rent (about $254m a year now, plus about $100m to $132m with Chicago, plus escalators); a largely fixed labour base (unionised); corporate costs (about $48m net a year); minimum technology and sponsorship payments.
  • Flow-through: about 55% on incremental Casinos & Resorts revenue (Change Forces digest).
  • Worked sensitivities (calculated; FY2026 consensus base: EBITDA $484m, EBIT $115m, pre-tax -$515m):
  • A 5% fall in Casinos & Resorts revenue (about $80m) at 55% flow-through lowers EBITDAR and EBITDA by about $44m: -9% to EBITDA, -38% to EBIT, and pre-tax loss about 9% wider.
  • A 1 percentage point fall in Casinos & Resorts margin costs about $16m of EBITDA (-3%).
  • The source's own figures: one point of group revenue growth is worth about $9.3m of EBITDAR; 0.5 points of margin about $15.9m, or $0.33 per share.
  • Chicago timing: rent starts at opening. If year one reaches only 60% of a $225m stabilised EBITDAR (my assumption for illustration), the casino earns about $135m against a pre-opening temporary contribution (undisclosed) and new rent of $100m to $132m. FY2027 EBITDA after rent could therefore be flat or down even if Chicago "opens on time", and the consensus EBITDA growth of +11.7% for FY2027 is at risk.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

F1225th of 25

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Bally's operating leverage has moved from inside its casinos to the rent line, and from there to the interest line. Over FY2020 to FY2025 a $1 change in revenue moved EBITDA after rent by roughly 55 cents in the casinos. From FY2027, the binding fact is different: rent plus interest (about $884m in FY2027 on my base) exceeds EBITDAR (about $761m), so the group sits below its fixed-charge break-even. In my base case EBITDAR covers rent plus interest only about 1.0 times in FY2026, and less than once in every year from FY2027 to FY2030. Over the next 18 months operating leverage works against every line below revenue. Revenue grows 5.7% in FY2027, yet EBITDA after rent falls about 5% ($483m to $459m) and pre-tax loss widens from about $376m to $523m. The cause is the step-up in fixed charges, not weak demand. Operating leverage only turns positive in FY2029, when Chicago's EBITDAR overtakes its new rent.

1. What is fixed, and how large it is (FY2026, my base):

Fixed or semi-fixed cost, USD m FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 Nature
Triple-net rent (GLPI) 248 302 346 Fixed, escalating 1% to 2% a year (CPI-linked, 1% floor), cross-defaulted, no deferral
Net interest (reported) 477 582 592 Fixed on about $1.0bn of swapped term loan to 2028; rest refinancing-driven
Corporate & Other EBITDAR loss 47 46 45 Largely fixed
Casino labour about 34% unionised, 36 collective agreements covering 3,679 workers Sticky
Rent plus interest 725 884 938
EBITDAR 731 761 832
EBITDAR / (rent + interest) 1.01x 0.86x 0.89x

The historical comparison: in FY2021 net interest was $118m and rent was small (most real estate was then owned), so fixed charges were about $140m. By FY2024 net interest was $304m and rent about $127m (Deutsche Bank), about $430m in total. In FY2026 the total is about $725m. Fixed charges have risen more than five times in five years, while EBITDAR has roughly doubled (FactSet operating EBITDA $340m in FY2021 against my $731m of EBITDAR in FY2026; calculated). That is why FY2019 to FY2021 profits (EBIT margins of 13% to 24%) became losses: the business was converted from owning its property to renting it, and from equity funding to debt funding.

2. Where operating leverage is positive, and how much:

  • Casinos & Resorts: about 55% flow-through on incremental revenue (first set; consistent with Q2 2026, when revenue rose $7.7m and EBITDAR $3.6m, about 47%). Each 1% of core casino revenue is about $14m of revenue and $7m to $8m of EBITDA, roughly $0.13 a share. The same leverage works in reverse: a 5% fall (about $70m on the core estate) costs about $38m of EBITDA, 8% of FY2027 EBITDA.
  • North America Interactive: now on variable-cost platforms (White Hat, Kambi). Incremental margins of 15% to 25% take segment EBITDAR from about zero in FY2026 to about $29m by FY2030 on my base. That is real but small, and 50% of it is shared with Bally's Intralot once cumulative breakeven is reached.
  • Bally's Intralot B2C (UK-led): the 40% duty cut the margin from 37.8% (Q2 2025) to 26.6% (Q2 2026). With 40% of each pound of gaming revenue now going to tax, incremental margins on UK growth fall from about 50% to about 30%. Operating leverage here is structurally lower than before April 2026.
  • Bally's Intralot B2B (lottery): contract-driven. Ohio's exit in mid-2027 removes about €20m a year of EBITDA. That is negative leverage on a largely fixed technology cost base.

3. Chicago: the key operating-leverage event, and why it is EBITDA-negative in its first year.

  • The rent: GLPI funds up to $940m of hard costs at an 8.5% yield. That adds about $80m a year of rent from opening. The first set used $100m to $132m. I disagree, because the $20m of Chicago land rent (since September 2024) and the $14.6m Tropicana ground rent are already in today's $248m. Jefferies' $132.1m is the total of all three.
  • The accounting step is larger than the cash step. GLPI's Q3 2026 10-Q shows $70.5m of deferred rental income on the Chicago funding, and Bally's carries a $222.7m prepaid rent asset. Bally's appears to be paying part of the construction-period rent in cash already. At opening, P&L rent may therefore rise by somewhat more than $80m (as the prepayment is released) while cash rent rises by less. This is my inference and is unconfirmed.
  • The ramp: my base has the permanent casino opening at the start of Q3 2027, one quarter later than the "spring 2027" guidance, given Bally's record of slippage. Stabilised net revenue is $500m and stabilised EBITDAR $150m. That is about 60% of Deutsche Bank's $250m, and the haircut covers the video gaming terminals, PENN's relocated Aurora and Joliet casinos, the hotel cut from 500 rooms to about 100, and a temporary casino earning about $13m of EBITDAR a year against a $50m target. In the first twelve months revenue is 65% of stabilised and the EBITDAR margin 20% (heavy promotional reinvestment to build the database, a high Illinois tax burden). National Harbor's first-year margin was 18.6% and Encore Boston Harbor's 23.2%.
  • The result, annualised against the temporary casino's roughly $13m:
Chicago, USD m Year 1 (from Q3 2027) Year 2 Year 3 Stabilised
EBITDAR 65 110 138 150
Less new rent -80 -81 -82 -84
Less temporary casino EBITDAR forgone -13 -13 -13 -13
Net change in EBITDA after rent -28 +16 +43 +53

Opening on time therefore lowers FY2027 EBITDA. A delay raises FY2027 EBITDA (no rent, the temporary casino keeps trading) but lowers cash (construction carry, possible liquidated damages from 10 December 2026, licence risk). The consensus FY2027 EBITDA growth of +11.7% therefore needs Chicago to earn EBITDAR well above its rent from the first quarter. That means roughly $150m to $250m annualised in year one, which is Deutsche Bank's model. The evidence (the temporary casino at about a quarter of its target, the video terminals, PENN's capacity) does not support it.

4. What it does to the main lines (my base against the past):

USD m FY2021 FY2023 FY2025 FY2026e FY2027e FY2028e FY2030e
Revenue 1,322 2,449 2,657 3,126 3,304 3,514 3,813
EBITDAR n/a n/a about 690 (inferred) 731 761 832 944
EBITDA after rent, brokers' basis (margin) n/a n/a 504 (19.0%) 483 (15.5%) 459 (13.9%) 486 (13.8%) 588 (15.4%)
EBIT (my basis) 180 (FactSet) -30 (FactSet) 29 (FactSet) 101 59 71 178
Pre-tax, adjusted -119 (reported) -186 (reported) -668 (reported) -376 -523 -521 -487

The EBITDA-after-rent margin, about 20% in FY2024, troughs at about 13.8% in FY2027 to FY2028. It recovers only to about 15.4% by FY2030, still below FY2025, because rent has risen by about $230m (FY2024 to FY2028) and the UK duty has removed about 10 points of B2C margin permanently.

5. The width of the range. Over the next 18 months the operating cone for FY2027 EBITDA after rent runs from about $380m to $610m around my $459m base. The low end has the UK leaking to unlicensed sites, Casinos & Resorts down 2% and sterling 8% weaker. The high end has Chicago opening in April 2027 on Deutsche Bank's $250m first-year EBITDAR. The upside rests almost entirely on one driver, the Chicago ramp. The downside is spread across several drivers (UK, regional consumer, currency, the B2B contracts). Because fixed charges absorb all of EBITDA, every $10m of EBITDA is about $0.16 a share, and the EPS cone runs from about $1.3 a share below my base to about $2.5 above it, before any capital-structure event.

For the Consumer: Model fixed charges as dated steps (Lincoln's rent already in; Chicago's $80m at opening; refinancing in Q1 2027) and treat Chicago's opening as EBITDA-dilutive in FY2027. The single most decision-relevant number is EBITDAR against rent plus interest, which is below 1.0x in every year of my base. Peers' equivalents are well above it: Boyd owns about 90% of its property at 2.5x leverage, PENN's lease-adjusted leverage is 5.3x to 5.7x, and Caesars' regional lease runs at about 1.0x rent coverage before interest.

5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-763P-024
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?F328th of 28full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

F425th of 25

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Financial leverage, not operations, sets Bally's EPS and balance sheet through FY2030. On the current capital structure the parent has a structural cash deficit of about $300m to $400m a year that does not close even after Chicago stabilises. In a debt-funded bridge each year's deficit adds roughly $40m to $60m to the next year's interest, so EPS does not converge to profit. My base EPS is about -$6.3 in FY2026, -$8.8 in FY2027 and between -$8.6 and -$8.9 in FY2028 to FY2030, against consensus of -$8.11, -$5.41 and -$5.26. Attributable book equity falls from about $2.1bn at end-2026 to roughly zero by end-2030. A capital-structure event (equity, debt-for-equity or an asset sale at the parent) is therefore not a tail. It is the probable path within 18 to 24 months. EPS forecasts beyond FY2027 are conditional statements, and their weight should be low.

1. The two-layer view (my inference; Bally's publishes no parent-only accounts):

USD m, my base FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030
Parent EBITDAR (Casinos & Resorts, North America Interactive, Corporate) 362 399 457 504 533
Rent (all at the parent) -248 -302 -346 -351 -357
Parent EBITDA after rent 114 97 111 152 177
Parent cash interest, excluding accretion and Bronx interest about -245 about -305 about -350 about -400 about -440
Parent maintenance and own development capex (ex Bronx) about -200 about -205 about -120 about -110 about -110
Dividends from Bally's Intralot (58% of €30m) +20 +20 +20 +20 +20
Parent funding need before the Bronx and before one-offs about -310 about -390 about -340 about -340 about -350

Chicago improves parent EBITDA after rent by only about $65m between FY2027 and FY2030. In the same period the debt-funded deficits add about $110m of interest. Chicago therefore does not de-lever the parent on any base-case ramp. Even Deutsche Bank's $250m stabilised EBITDAR would add only about another $100m. That is the main difference from the first set's framing of Chicago as the "positive deleveraging loop". It helps, but it is too small for the gap.

2. The interest path (reported, consolidated) and why:

  • FY2026: -$477m. H1 actual $228.9m; Q3 about $118m; Q4 about $130m, including the WhiteHawk Bronx loan ($400m at SOFR plus 8.50%, from October 2026).
  • FY2027: -$582m. Of this, Bally's Intralot is about $162m (€1,761m of debt; H1 2026 net finance €69.6m), and the parent ex-Bronx is about $320m, of which about $85m is non-cash accretion. On top of that, a covenant reset and repricing of the term loan and revolver adds about 250bp on about $1.4bn ($26m for nine months), $450m of new secured private credit at about 13% from Q2 2027 adds $44m, and the half of Bronx interest not yet capitalised adds $30m.
  • FY2028 to FY2030: -$592m, -$632m and -$665m. Further debt-funded deficits of about $330m a year at 13%, plus refinancing the 5.625% 2029 notes at about 12%. Bronx interest is capitalised from 2028 until opening (after 2030 in my base), so it sits in capex, not interest. Bally's Intralot's interest drifts down as it de-levers without Evoke.
  • Against consensus: consensus FY2027 implies about $464m of net interest and other items (EBIT $119m against pre-tax -$345m). That is roughly today's run-rate, with no repricing, no Bronx and no new money. My figure is $118m higher. That gap alone is about $1.95 a share.

3. EPS sensitivity to financial leverage:

  • Refinancing spread: each 100bp on about $3.0bn of parent debt is about $30m, or $0.49 a share. Each $100m of extra debt-funded deficit at 13% is $13m, or $0.21 a share, the following year.
  • Floating rates: low exposure until 2028, because $1.0bn of swaps fix the term loan through 2028 and a $500m cross-currency swap fixes 6.69% in euros to October 2028. The sterling swap expired in October 2026. The risk is refinancing spread, not SOFR.
  • Equity gearing: enterprise value of about $6.8bn against a market capitalisation of about $0.85bn makes the equity about eight times geared. A 10% change in enterprise value is roughly an 80% change in equity value.

4. The balance sheet path (my base, approximate):

USD m Mid-2026 (actual) End-2026 End-2027 End-2028 End-2030
Consolidated net debt excluding leases about 4,420 about 4,800 about 5,500 about 6,000 about 7,000
Rent capitalised at 8x about 2,000 about 2,000 about 2,400 about 2,800 about 2,900
Rent-adjusted net debt / EBITDAR (consolidated) about 9x about 9.3x about 10.4x about 10.6x about 10.5x
Parent only, rent-adjusted (inference) about 13x about 15x to 16x about 15x about 14x
Attributable book equity about 2,350 about 2,100 about 1,550 about 1,000 about 0

Intangible assets alone were $2.86bn at June 2026, so tangible book equity is already negative. The parent-only rent-adjusted leverage of 13x to 16x agrees with Deutsche Bank's 14.0x pro forma. Bally's Intralot, ring-fenced, should drift from 4.05x towards about 3.5x by FY2028 without Evoke, and up to about 4.5x with it.

5. EPS under each capital-structure branch (FY2027, my inference):

Branch (18-month probability) Mechanics FY2027 EPS Note
A. Debt-funded bridge (45%) Reset plus about $450m at 13% about -8.8 Base case
B. Standard General-backed equity recapitalisation (20%) About $500m raised at about $8 from mid-2027, about 62m new shares about -5.4 Smaller loss per share, but existing holders own about half as much; FY2028 about -4.0
C. Take-private by Standard General (10%) Squeeze-out of the roughly 33% float not applicable Price set by the majority; the 2024 bid was 7.8 times forward EV/EBITDA
D. Restructuring, in or out of court (20%) Debt-for-equity; GLPI rent protected by cross-default not meaningful Station Casinos took about 2 years and Caesars' operating company nearly 3; equity heavily diluted or wiped out
E. Clean refinancing below a 10% blended cost (5%) The first set's "change my mind" trigger about -8.0 Requires credit markets to reopen; euro high-yield is up 90bp since September

The paradox is that the most dilutive survival branch (B) shows the least negative EPS. Per-share EPS comparisons across branches are therefore misleading. The Consumer should compare enterprise value and the claim of each security on it, not EPS.

For the Consumer: Treat EPS beyond FY2027 as conditional on a capital-structure event that my base case does not yet embed. Use the parent funding need (about $300m to $400m a year) as the variable to monitor. Compare forecasts with consensus on interest first: the $118m FY2027 interest gap is larger than the $82m EBITDA gap.

5d

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7715P-0322
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

F1528th of 28

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Little of the cost base that matters is cuttable. The largest items (rent and interest, roughly $730m a year) are contractually fixed and can only be reduced through a restructuring. The cuttable pool (corporate, marketing, some labour) is perhaps $30m to $60m a year at the parent (my inference), small against the gap.

  • Rent (GLPI master leases, about $254m rising to $350m-plus): not cuttable outside a restructuring. The leases forbid deferral, and GLPI holds cross-default protection.
  • Interest (about $476m consolidated; about $300m at the parent): reducible only by deleveraging through asset sales or equity, or by a debt exchange.
  • Casinos & Resorts operating costs: labour is about 34% unionised and sticky. Some promotional reinvestment can be cut on lower tiers, already done. Property maintenance has already been squeezed (Deutsche Bank).
  • Corporate (about $48m a year net): partly cuttable. About $15m of savings is already claimed; an interim CFO who is also President saves little.
  • North America Interactive: variable platform fees (White Hat, Kambi) and marketing are cuttable, but cutting further would shrink revenue.
  • Development: the hotel and non-gaming spend are deferrable (already paused); the Chicago minimum spend is mandated by the host agreement; the Bronx can be slowed only at the risk of the licence.
  • Bally's Intralot: €41m of synergies, the €50m mitigation plan and £180m from Evoke are cuttable at the subsidiary, and only 58% of them is Bally's.
P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

F2225th of 25

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Real but small cost savings will reach the lines: about $30m to $45m of EBITDA in FY2027 against FY2026, and about $60m to $80m of run-rate by FY2028. Most of it is in the 58%-owned Bally's Intralot (merger synergies and the UK duty mitigation plan), and it is already in my segment margins. It offsets inflation and the UK duty rather than lifting group margins. The costs that matter, rent and interest (about $725m in FY2026, rising to $884m in FY2027), cannot be cut outside a restructuring. Precedent says landlords do not cut rent out of court, and restructurings take two to three years. At the parent, the cuttable pool is about $30m to $60m. That is a tenth of the fixed-charge gap.

1. What reaches the lines, by source and date (my base):

Source Claimed or available Reaches EBITDA (USD m) When Confidence
"Bally's 2.0" corporate and casino overhead over $15m a year (November 2025) Corporate & Other EBITDAR loss fell from $17.5m to $11.8m in Q2 2026 (about $23m annualised); I hold about -$45m to -$47m a year flat, because the gain is already in the base and inflation offsets more Already in FY2026 High that it is achieved; low that more comes
Bally's Intralot merger synergies €41m (€15m in 2026, rising to €25m in 2027, full run-rate later) about +$12m to +$15m more in FY2027 and +$10m in FY2028, at 100% 2026 to 2028 Medium
UK duty mitigation plan €50m a year (€25m less player generosity, €10m core costs, €15m synergies; 65% of the Q2 2026 duty hit already offset) about +$15m to +$20m more in FY2027 (first full year) H2 2026 to H1 2027 Medium; the player response to the generosity cuts is untested
North America Interactive (variable platforms, automation, CRM) breakeven reached Q2 2026 +$9m in FY2027, +$16m in FY2028 at 100%; half shared with Bally's Intralot 2027 to 2030 Medium
Casino labour and amenities (post-COVID playbook: buffets, poker rooms, cashless) peers cut labour 20% to 25% after 2020 Little left; Bally's already did this. I assume casino labour inflation of about $10m to $15m a year outweighs it, so the core margin falls 30bp in FY2027 Medium
Deferred maintenance capex maintenance about 4.4% of casino revenue against a 6% to 8% industry norm (Morgan Stanley) Not a saving: it shows up later as share loss (P-05) High

Net, the FY2027 consolidated EBITDAR margin is about flat (23.4% to 23.0%), and the savings offset the full-year UK duty, the Ohio exit and wage inflation.

2. What cannot be cut, and the precedents:

  • Rent ($248m, rising to $346m by FY2028). The GLPI master leases are cross-defaulted, escalate at a 1% minimum and forbid deferral. VICI and GLPI collected 100% of cash rent through the 2020 closures (Fitch). Out-of-court concessions have taken the form of capex relief (Caesars-VICI, June 2020) or asset swaps and sales (Century Casinos shed about $7.5m of rent by selling properties in 2026). There has been no rent forgiveness. The Caesars-VICI regional lease at about 1.0x coverage is in talks for a possible rent reduction (Jefferies, 11 January 2026), which is the first real test. Bally's own leases are covered 1.98x and 2.13x at the property level (Deutsche Bank, November 2026), so GLPI has no reason to cut.
  • Interest ($477m, rising to about $582m). It can be cut only by deleveraging (asset sales, equity) or restructuring. Caesars' operating company cut annual interest about 75% (from $1.7bn to about $450m) through Chapter 11, filed January 2015 and emerged October 2017. Station Casinos took about two years (2009 to 2011). Both turned debt into rent or equity. Neither cut was quick or kind to the old equity.
  • Development. The Chicago hotel and about 12% of amenities are already paused (deferral, not elimination). The Bronx can be slowed only at the risk of the licence. The cheapest "cost cut" available is to stop buying things: Sam's Town, Bally's Intralot shares ($42.7m in H1 2026), the Star stake.

3. Timing against the past. Bally's cost programmes have delivered: the 2023 interactive restructuring ($36.8m of charges, 15% of the interactive workforce), the move to variable-cost platforms, and Bally's 2.0. But each was overtaken by new fixed charges (sale-leasebacks, 11% notes, the Bronx). The pattern from FY2022 to FY2026 is cost-out at the operating line and cost-in at the financing line. I expect the same through FY2028.

4. Is a "large cut" plausible? Only through a restructuring (branch D, 20%), which could remove $150m to $250m of parent interest. It would come in 2028 at the earliest, with equity largely diluted. An asset sale of part of the Bally's Intralot stake or of the Star stake could cut $20m to $40m of interest. Neither is in my base, because Bally's has said it wants to increase its Bally's Intralot stake, and the term loan is secured on that equity.

For the Consumer: Do not add cost savings on top of my segment margins; they are already in them. Treat any announced "cost programme" at the parent as at most $10m to $20m. The levers that would change the forecasts are rent and interest, and they move only in a capital-structure event.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4718P-0514
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?F1828th of 28full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

F1425th of 25

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: The dominant loop over the next 18 to 36 months is financial, not operational. A funding deficit is met with expensive debt, which raises interest, which widens the next deficit. This loop adds about $40m to $60m of interest a year for each year it runs, and it is the reason my EPS does not converge to profit. The AlphaSense report frames the question as the underinvestment spiral against the Chicago-ramp deleveraging loop. My answer: the underinvestment spiral is real but slow (about 0.5 to 1 point a year off core casino revenue), and the Chicago loop is balancing, not reinforcing, until FY2029, because its rent arrives before its earnings. The only clearly positive reinforcing loop, UK consolidation, runs inside the ring-fenced subsidiary and is capped by offshore leakage. Two balancing loops keep the company alive rather than improving it: GLPI's interest in a 2x-covered tenant, and Standard General's interest in its control. Net, the loops subtract about $40m to $60m from EBITDA and about $150m to $200m from pre-tax profit by FY2028, against a static no-loop path.

1. The loops, step by step, with their effect on the lines (my base):

Loop Type Mechanism Evidence it is running now Effect in my base
1. Financing spiral Reinforcing, negative Parent deficit (about $300m to $400m a year) → new secured debt at 12% to 13% → interest +$40m to +$60m → bigger deficit Going-concern note (August 2026); WhiteHawk Bronx loan at SOFR plus 850; unsecured bonds at yields up to 12.9%; GLPI's 29 October 2026 warning Net interest rises from $477m (FY2026) to $665m (FY2030) despite EBITDA after rent rising about $105m
2. Underinvestment and share loss Reinforcing, negative, slow Fixed charges → maintenance capex below the 6% to 8% norm → older floors and technology → share lost to refreshed rivals → lower EBITDAR → less capex Capex fell to $199.8m (2024) from $311.5m (2023); Deutsche Bank: leverage "prevents [Bally's] from investing in legacy property maintenance"; Missouri -30bp (August 2026); Atlantic City decline; a former competitor on lost loyalty from slow floor technology (May 2024); app downloads +12% against Boyd's +146% Core casino revenue at 0% (FY2027) and +0.5% to +1% (FY2028 to FY2030), against +1.5% to +2% for a healthy regional operator; core margin -30bp and -20bp. Cost by FY2028: about $15m to $25m of EBITDA
3. Landlord loop Reinforcing, negative for Bally's; balancing for solvency Sale-leasebacks raise cash now and fixed rent forever → higher break-even → more sales needed. GLPI also funds development and protects a covered tenant Rent from about $127m (2024) to $248m (2026) to $346m (2028); Lincoln +$56m; Kansas City and Shreveport +$32.2m; Chicago +$80m; GLPI funded $640.7m and warned publicly Rent is the largest single drag on the EBITDA-after-rent margin (about -5 points FY2024 to FY2028). Balancing side: GLPI's next move is more likely to be funding or restructuring support than termination, because its two Bally's lease pools are 1.98x and 2.13x covered and replacement operators are interested (Deutsche Bank, 2 November 2026)
4. Chicago ramp Balancing in FY2027 to FY2028; weakly reinforcing positive from FY2029 Opening → rent at once → EBITDAR ramps over 2 to 3 years → database growth → better ramp Topped off July 2026; temporary casino at about $13m of EBITDAR a year against a $50m target; Q2 2026 temporary revenue $37.5m Net EBITDA after rent -$28m in year one, +$16m, +$43m, then +$53m at stabilisation (P-01). It does not de-lever the parent (P-02)
5. Chicago politics Reinforcing, negative Video gaming terminals legalised → lower projected casino revenue → hotel paused → lower city tax receipts → more political intervention (property-tax relief opposed, online sports-betting tax up, a proposed table-game tax rise in the Illinois FY2027 budget) August 2026 legalisation; Bally's projects a $260m tax loss (advocacy, not a forecast); Accel 44% of approvals Inside my Chicago haircut (stabilised revenue $500m against Deutsche Bank's $703m). Downside if the dispute escalates to licence or liquidated-damages action after 10 December 2026
6. UK consolidation Reinforcing, positive, but capped The 40% duty pushes sub-scale operators out → their players move to scaled operators → acquisition costs fall → better margins → more capacity to buy (Evoke) Tier 1 +7 points of share since 2021; Bally's Intralot's UK share stable at 14% to 15% with 11% marketing intensity; UK +11.6% in Q2 2026 B2C revenue +2.8% (FY2027) and +4% a year after; margin recovers to about 30%
6a. Offshore leakage Balancing against loop 6 Lower generosity → high-value players go to unlicensed sites UK offshore gross gambling yield tripled to £685m since 2019; Netherlands -25% Caps loop 6. In the bear case B2C revenue falls about 4% in FY2027
7. Parent-subsidiary Reinforcing, negative for the subsidiary; weakly positive for parent cash Parent need → dividends and intercompany charges → slower subsidiary deleveraging €30m dividend (58% to Bally's); payables to Bally's up to €30.31m from €11.74m; Bally's Intralot covenant 4.75x; Fitch B+ on Rating Watch Negative I hold dividends at about $20m a year to the parent. The covenant ring-fence limits more. Material only if Standard General decides to sell down or pledge the stake
8. Controlling shareholder Balancing (it backstops solvency) but value-transferring Distress → Standard General acts (equity, debt, take-private) on its own terms $38, $15 and $18.25 bids; Executive Chair since January 2026; stated intent to buy Bally's Intralot shares Drives branches B and C (30% combined) in P-02
9. North America Interactive operating leverage Reinforcing, positive, small Variable platform costs → profit on growth → reinvestment in CRM Q2 2026 +$3.0m EBITDAR, revenue +16.9% Segment EBITDAR from about 0 to about $29m by FY2030; half shared with Bally's Intralot

2. Which loop dominates, and when.

  • Next 18 months: loop 1 (financing) and loop 3 (rent step-up) dominate. They take FY2027 pre-tax from about -$376m to about -$523m while EBITDAR rises. Loop 4 is negative in FY2027.
  • 18 to 36 months: loop 4 turns mildly positive, but loop 1 compounds faster unless a capital-structure event breaks it. A restructuring (branch D) or a large equity injection (branch B) is the only thing that reverses loop 1. Operations cannot.
  • Precedent: Station Casinos (2009) and Caesars' operating company (2015) show the same order of events: development plus leverage, then a financing loop, then restructuring, with operations intact and the property owner protected. Revel shows the extreme case, where a new urban casino could not carry its capital structure through the ramp. PENN's Joliet and Aurora relocations show what the positive loop looks like when it is funded and owned (volumes up 1.4 to 2 times, about a 15% cash-on-cash return). Bally's Chicago has the asset but not the funding.

3. Interplay that raises predictability risk. The loops are correlated. A weak Chicago ramp worsens the financing loop and the political loop at the same time. A UK leakage shock weakens the subsidiary just as the parent may need to draw on it. The cone is therefore fat-tailed on the downside. The only offset is loop 8 (Standard General), which helps solvency but not minority value.

For the Consumer: For predictability work, monitor loop 1 through the terms of any refinancing before 31 March 2027 (amount, coupon, security, any equity). Monitor loop 2 through monthly state gross gaming revenue share in Missouri, Atlantic City, Shreveport and Rhode Island, and through the maintenance capex line. Monitor loop 6 through Bally's Intralot's UK constant-currency growth on 27 November 2026.

To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

  • The subsidiary's FY2026 guidance: adjusted EBITDA of about €422m (reaffirmed in May 2026), against Deutsche Bank's €390m (about 8% below) and Deutsche Bank's earlier baseline of €423m before the results. Its FY2027 pro forma target is €454m.
  • The tension inside that guidance: the CEO's Interactive outlook of about €280m to €295m, plus legacy lottery EBITDA of about €104m annualised (H1 2026 €52.0m), sums to about €384m to €399m, below the €422m guidance unless the second half improves or the guidance includes items not visible. That is my inference; the subsidiary's Key Question work raises the same consistency question. The guidance is therefore probably at risk of being rebased at its Q3 results on 27 November 2026 or with its FY2026 results.
  • Why the gap exists: management assumes full UK mitigation, the €15m of 2026 synergies and a second-half pickup in US lottery jackpots, while the sell-side discounts the duty and contract losses.

Case simplicity

How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

North America Interactive operates in several states. Bally's Intralot B2C covers the UK, Spain and Turkey (Bilyoner), with Evoke pending (the UK, Italy, Spain, Denmark and Romania). Group judgement

Risks

F2815th of 15

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The third set asks whether the financial forecasts for the next 18 months are predictable. The answer is that the operating forecasts are more predictable than usual for a company this complex and the equity forecasts are less predictable than for almost any company in the cohort, and the two statements are not in tension: they are the same fact seen from above and below the fixed-charge line. Above it, the drivers are few, dated and observable (D-03): three of the four (rent steps, the repricing of debt, the UK duty) are written in leases, credit agreements and statute, and the fourth (Chicago's ramp) will be visible monthly from the Illinois Gaming Board and quarterly from Bally's Chicago, Inc. within weeks of opening (D-05). The earlier sets agree with each other and the new evidence agrees with them (D-01); our base case rests on strengths Bally's has demonstrated and discounts the one it has not (D-02). The FY2027 operating cone, roughly $380m to $610m of EBITDA after rent around $459m, is wide but tractable, and its skew is explicable: the probability sits below the base and the magnitude above it, because the one big upside is a single binary driver (D-04). Below the line, nothing is predictable in the ordinary sense. The interest line depends on terms that private-credit lenders will set by 31 March 2027; the share count and the claim on the enterprise depend on which of five capital-structure branches occurs; the company discloses nothing about either; and the per-share unit itself is unreliable because the brokers' bases span $4 a share on the same EBITDA (D-06). The share price confirms where the uncertainty lives: it moves with financing headlines and not with its sector (1% explained by peers), from a thin float, in gaps (D-07).

Three judgements follow for the memo. First, the predictability question has a different answer for each of the memo's audiences: a creditor or landlord can forecast this company adequately (property-level coverage of about 2x, dated rent and interest, observable receipts); a minority equity holder cannot, because the equity's value is set by negotiations it is not party to, with a sponsor whose own capital is too small to rescue it alone and whose record is of transactions on its own terms. Second, the next six months contain most of the information that would narrow the cone (Q3 2026 results and the liquidity test; Bally's Intralot on 27 November; the Chicago deadline on 10 December; the FY2026 10-K and the refinancing by March 2027), so the memo should treat today's forecasts as provisional on those dates and say which print would change which number. Third, the combination of a predictable operating business and an unpredictable claim is the signature of a restructuring candidate, not of an investment case in the ordinary sense; the fair comparison set is Station Casinos in 2008 and Caesars' operating company in 2014, not PENN or Boyd. My branch weights (a bridge 40%, a sponsor-led recapitalisation 20%, a restructuring 30%, a take-private 5%, a clean refinancing 5%) put roughly a 55% to 60% probability on the common equity being worth less, and possibly far less, in 18 months, against a 5% to 10% probability of a sharp re-rating from a sponsor or exchange event, with the remainder a survival at today's value with a bigger claim pending. That is a case whose forecasts are predictable in direction and unpredictable in the one number Richard needs, the value of the share.

D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

F2515th of 15

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: It depends on whose expected change is being tested. Our base case relies almost entirely on strengths Bally's has demonstrated (a stable regional estate, a UK business that has mitigated the duty better than any peer, a North America Interactive unit that reached breakeven, a landlord that keeps funding) and discounts the one it has not (opening and ramping a flagship urban casino on time), so our forecast is plausible on the record. Consensus and management's informal milestones rely on three strengths Bally's would need to acquire: delivering a large development on schedule (its record is of months and quarters of slippage), ramping an urban casino at or above the sell-side's first-year assumptions (the temporary casino ran at a quarter of its own target and below the City of Chicago's projection), and refinancing on better terms than the last financing (every financing since 2024 has been dearer than the one before). The precedent evidence is unambiguous on the fourth required strength, closing a gap between earnings and fixed charges from lease-adjusted leverage above 7x with a development under way: the AlphaSense search found no operator that did so without an equity injection, a large asset sale or a restructuring. For predictability, the balance is negative: the single driver that sets the width of the FY2027 outcome (Chicago) is the undemonstrated one, and the driver that sets the equity outcome (financing) depends on counterparties, not on Bally's.

1. The expected changes, tested one by one.

Expected change (whose) Demonstrated strength it rests on Strength it would need to acquire Evidence and plausibility
Chicago opens in H1 2027 and earns $160m to $250m of EBITDAR in year one (consensus, Deutsche Bank, management) Topping-out in July 2026; GLPI funding on schedule ($640.7m of $940m); a 113,000-member database; landside conversions worked at Baton Rouge (+590% gaming revenue) and Marquette On-time delivery of a $1.7bn urban project; an urban ramp above peers; selling into a market where video terminals go live about when the casino opens The temporary casino opened in September 2023 against a June target, delivered $13.1m of EBITDAR against a $50m target, and $125m of receipts in 2024 against the city's $243m projection; the permanent opening slipped from September 2026 to late 2026 to spring 2027; the hotel is paused; "two-thirds complete" in November 2026 for an H1 2027 opening is tight. Urban precedents are split: National Harbor beat its underwriting ($637m annualised receipts in its first weeks; $195m of EBITDA by 2018), Encore Boston ramped slowly, Catskills ran at about 10% margins and needed equity, Revel failed. Illinois relocations ramp fast (Joliet +68% cumulative receipts, Aurora +84% first month, Rockford +50% in 2025) but they are suburban, highway-adjacent and were owned by investment-grade-adjacent tenants.
